  \par Find $u_0\in\mathcal{C}^\infty_{0,q-1}(D)$ such that $\overline{\partial}u_0 =f $ on $D_0$. Assume $u_0,u_1,\cdots,u_d$ are such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \cpd u_i = f\text{ on $D_i$}, u_i = u_{i-1}\text{ on $D_{i-2}$}.
  \end{equation*}
Find $u_{d+1}'\in\mathcal{C}^\infty_{0,q-1}(D)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd u'_{d+1} = f\on D_d.
\end{equation*}
Find $v\in\mathcal{C}^\infty_{0,q-2}(D)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd v = u'_{d+1}-u_d\on D_{d-1}.
\end{equation*}
Set $u_{d+1} = u'_{d+1}-\cpd v$ Then we have,
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd u_{d+1} = f\on D_{d+1}.
\end{equation*}
Furthermore,
\begin{equation*}
  u_{d+1}-u_d = u'_{d+1}-\cpd v-u_d = 0\on D_{d-1}.
\end{equation*}
Inductively, we get $u_0,u_1,\cdots$. Set $u = \lim_{d\to\infty}u_d$. On $D_k$ we have 
\begin{equation*}
u \equiv u_d, \text{$d$ sufficiently large}, \cpd u = \cpd u_d = f.
\end{equation*}
Assume that we found $u_0,u_1,u_2,\cdots,u_d$ with 
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd u_i = f\on D_i, \vert u_i-u_{i-1}\vert_{D_{i-2}} \leq {\frac 1 {2^i}}.
\end{equation*}
Construct $u_{d+1}$ by taking $u'_{d+1}\in\mathcal{C}^\infty(D)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd u'_{d+1} = f\on D_{d+1}.
\end{equation*}
We also have 
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd(\underbrace{u'_{d+1}-u_d}_{\text{\makebox[0pt][c]{a holomorphic function on $D_d$}}}) = 0\on D_d.
\end{equation*}
Let $p$ be a Taylor polynomial of $u'_{d+1}-u_d$ about $0$ degree chosen sufficiently large so that 
\begin{equation*}
  \vert u'_{d+1}-u_d - p|_{D_d-1}<{\frac 1 {2^{l+1}}}.
\end{equation*}
Set $u_{d+1} = u'_{d+1}-p$. Continus this procedure get a sequence $u_0,u_1,\cdots$. Take $u= \lim_{d\to\infty}u_d$. We observe this is locally uniform. Therefore, $u$ is continuous.
\par Consider $u-u_m$ then 
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{\substack{d\to\infty\\ d>m}}(\overbrace{u_d-u_m}^{\text{\makebox[0pt][c]{holomorphic function for $d$ large enough}}}).
\end{equation*}
Thus $u$ is of $\cinf$. In particular,
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd u = \underbrace{\cpd(u-u_m)}_{=0} +\cpd u_m = f.
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  In the above proof, we can assume the solution $u$ is $\mathcal{C}^\infty_{0,q-1}(D')$ but $\overline{\partial}u=f$ is true only on $D''$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Dolbeault Cohomology]
  Let $D$ be a polydisk. We define, the Dolbeault cohomology as
  \begin{equation*}
    H_{\Dol}^{0,q}(D) = {\frac {\{ f\in\cinf_{0,q}\sep \cpd f = 0\}} {\{\cpd u\sep u\in\cinf_{0,q}\}}}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}


\begin{remark}
  Theorem \ref{lemma_dolbeault} can be restated by the following way.
  \begin{equation*}
    H^{0,q}_{\Dol}(D) = 0,
  \end{equation*}
  for any $q\geq1$. This is proved by first showing the case when $q=1$, then b the induction for higher cases.
\end{remark}

For $n=1$ and $\G$ be an arbitrary domain. Suppose we want to solve the equation,
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{cases}
    \cpd u = f,\\
    \pd{u(z)}{\cz} = f(z)
  \end{cases}.
\end{equation*}

Take $\G_0\compembed\G_1\compembed\cdots\compembed\G$ such that it exhausts $\G$. Suppose $\bd\G$ is reasonable(ie. the Stoke's theorem can be applied). 
Solve $u_j\in\cinf(\G)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
\pd{u_j}{\cz_j}(z) = f(z)\on \G_j.
\end{equation*}
To do so, we need an approximation theorem. Namely, the Range Approximation Theorem. Then it follows that 
\begin{equation*}
  \exists u\in\cinf(\G), \pd{u}{\cz} = f.
\end{equation*}
$u$ was not constructed by a linear operator, 
\begin{equation*}
\cinf_{0,q}\ni f\stackrel{T}{\mapsto}Tf\in\cinf_{0,q-1},\cpd Tf = f.
\end{equation*}
Using Functional analysis, we know that such $T$ does not exists. Let $f\in\cinf_{0,q},\cpd f= 0$, bounded on $D$.
\subsection{Applying the Cauchy-Riemann equation}

\begin{definition}[Meromorphic functions]
  Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be open. $f:\G\to\C$ is meromorphpic if for any $z\in\G$ there is a neighborhood $U$ such that there are $g,h\in\Ouv(U)$ with,
  \begin{equation*}
    \forall z\in U\backslash h^{-1}(0), f(z) = {\frac {g(z)} {h(z)}}.
  \end{equation*} 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $U$ be an open set. We denote $\mathcal{K}(U)$ to be the set of meromorphic functions on $U$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  We have $\Ouv_x\subset\mathcal{K}_x$ and $\mathcal{K}(U) = \bigsqcup_{x\in U}\mathcal{K}_x$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a domain. Cousin-$I$-distribution is $(U_i,f_i)_{i\in I}$, such that 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item $(U_i)_{i\in I}$ is an open cover of $\G$,
    \item $f_i\in\mathcal{K}(U_i)$ is a meromorphic function for each $i\in I$ such that $f_j-f_i = f_{ij}$ is a holomorphic function on $U_{ij}=U_i\cap U_j$.
  \end{enumerate}
  A solution to such a distribution is a meromorphic function $f$ on $\G$ such that $f-f_i$ is holomorphic on $U_i$ for each $i\in I$.
\end{definition}

We now want to find a meromorphic function $f$ on $\G$ such that $f-f_i$ is holomorphic on $U_i$.

\begin{definition}
  A divisor of $\G$ is a pair $\Delta=(U_i,f_i)_{i\in I}$ such that 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item $(U_i)_{i\in I}$ is an open covering of $\G$,
    \item $f_i\not\equiv0$ is meromorphic in $U_i$ for each $i\in I$,
    \item $f_j/f_i = f_{ij}$ is holomorhpic without zeros on $U_{ij}$ for each pair $i,j\in I$. 
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\Delta_1,\Delta_2$ be two divisors. They are said to coincide if $\Delta_1\cup\Delta_2$ is also a divisor. 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  A divisor of the form $(\G,f)$ is called principle.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  A solution of such a $\Delta=(U_i,f_i)_{i\in I}$ is a meromorphic function $f$ on $\G$ with $f/f_i$ is holomorphic without zeros on $U_i$. That is a meromorphic function $f$ on $\G$ such that $\Delta$ and $(\G,f)$ concincide.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Second Cousin Problem]
  Given a divisor $\Delta$ of $\G$, the solution of the second Cousin problem is a principle diisor $(\G,f)$ that coincides with $\Delta$.
\end{definition}
  \begin{definition}[Third Cousin Problem]
    The third Cousin problem is solvable on a domain $\G$ containing the origin, if the set
    \begin{equation*}
      M_0 = \{f\in\Ouv(\G)\sep f(0)=0\},
    \end{equation*}
    is the ideal $(z_1,\cdots,z_n)$. That is for any $f\in\Ouv(\G)$, the Diophantine equation,
    \begin{equation*}
    \sum_{j=1}^n f_j(z)z_j = f(z),
  \end{equation*}
  is solvable.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{definition}[Fourth Cousin Problem]
    Let $\G$ be a domain, $M\subseteq\G$ be an analytic set and $f\in\Ouv(M)$. A solution of the fourth cousin problem of $f$ is a holomorphic function $F\in\Ouv(\G)$ such that $F|_M=f$.
  \end{definition}
\begin{definition}[Fifth Cousin Problem]
  Let $\G$ be a domain and $M\subseteq\G$ be an analytic set of codimension $1$. The fifth Cousin problem is solvable on $M$ if there is $f\in\Ouv(\G)$ such that $V(f) = M$. 
\end{definition}
  $(U_i,f_i)_{i\in I}$ be a principal part distribution such that $U_i\compembed \G$. Let $\{q_i\}_{i\in I}$ be a partition of unity subordinate to $\{U_i\}_{i\in I}$. Set 
  \begin{equation*}
    g_i = \sum\varphi_if_{ji}\on U_i,\text{ where } f_{ji} = f_i-f_j\on U_{ij}.
  \end{equation*}
  Then we have,
  \begin{align*}
    \cpd g_i - \cpd g_k &= \cpd\sum\varphi_jf_{ji}-\cpd\sum\varphi_j f_{jk},\\
& = \cpd\sum\varphi_j(f_{ji}-f_{jk}),\\
& = \cpd\sum_j f_{ki}\varphi_j,\\
&= f_{ki}\underbrace{\sum\cpd\varphi_j}_{\mathrlap{\!\text{$=0$ as $\cpd$ is a closed $(0,1)$ form}}} = 0.
  \end{align*}
  Set $r = \cpd g_i$ on $U_i$. Globally defined on all of $\G$.

\par Assume Cauchy-Riemann equation is solvable, that is there is $u\in\cinf(\G)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  \cpd u = r.
\end{equation*}
Set $f=f_i-g_i+u$ on $U_i$. Then $\cpd f = 0$. Note that $f$ is globally defined. Indeed,
\begin{equation*}
  (f_i-g_i+u)-(f_k-g_k+u) = f_i-f_k-(g_i-g_k) = 0.
\end{equation*}
Particularly, $f-f_i$ is holomorphic thus $f$ solves the First Cousin problem. That is on a Disk, the First cousin problem is solvable.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{theorem_2_2_cr}
  If $(\CR)_{0,q}$ is solvable then $M_0 = (z_1,\cdots,z_n)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
   Let $\G$ be a domain in $\C^n$ and $a\in\bd\G$. A boundary poitn $c\in \bd\G$ is called attainable if there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that for some $b\in B_\varepsilon(a)\cap \G$, and $\delta$ the Euclidean distance between $b$ and $\bd\G$, we have, $c\in \bd B_\delta(b)\cap\bd\G\cap B_{2\varepsilon}(a)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  For each attainable boundary point $c$, we find a holomorphic function on $\G$ which is not extendable beyond $c$. 
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
  Consider $\G\cap\{z_n=0\} = \G_0$ not empty and $0\in\bd\G$. Consider $j:\G_0\hookrightarrow\G$ and $p:\C^n\to\C^{n-1}$. Consider 
  \begin{equation*}
    M = \{z\in\G\sep p(z)\not\in\G_0\}.
  \end{equation*}
  This is relatively closed in $\G$. Also $\G_0$ is relatively closed in $\G$. We have $M\cap\G_0=\empty$. So there isa $\cinf$ function on $\G$,$\varphi:\G\to[0,1]$ with $\varphi \equiv1$ on $V(\G_0)$ and $\varphi\equiv0$ on $M$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{theorem_2_3_cr}If $\G$ is such that $(\CR)_{0,q}$ are always solvable then $\G$ is a domain of holomorphy.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$. The proof is due to induction. For $n=1$, we have that all domains are holomorphically convex. Suppose we have the case for $n-1$.
  \par Let $a\in\bd\G$ and $\G_0 = \{z_n=0\}\cap\G$. Without loss of generality, we assume that $0\in\bd\G_0$ in $\C^{n-1}$. Let 
  \begin{equation*}
    p:\C^n\to\C^{n-1},j:\G_0\to\G,
  \end{equation*}
  be canonical projection and injection, respectively. We set,
  \begin{equation*}
    M\defeq \{z\in\G\sep p(z)\not\in\G_0\}.
  \end{equation*}
  Then $M,\G_0$ are closed in $\G$ and $M\cap \G_0 = \emptyset$. Take $\varphi\in\cinf(\G)$ such that $0\leq \varphi\leq 1$ and
  \begin{equation*}
    \varphi(\G_0) = \{1\}, \varphi(M) = \{0\}.
  \end{equation*}
  We first show that $f\in\cinf_{0,q}(\G_0), \overline{\bd} f = 0$ then there is $F\in\cinf_{0,q}(\G)$ such that $\overline{\bd}F=0$ and $F|_{\G_0} = f$ that is $F\circ j = f$.
  \par Indeed $(\varphi(f\circ p))\circ j=f$. Therefore take 
  \begin{equation*}
    F = \varphi(f\circ p) - z_nu,
  \end{equation*}
  where $u\in\cinf_{0,q}(\G)$ so that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \overline{\partial}F = 0.
  \end{equation*}
  That is 
  \begin{align*}
    0 & = \overline{\partial}F = \overline{\partial}\varphi\circ(f\circ p) - z_n\overline{\partial}v.
    \Rightarrow & \overline{\partial}u = {\frac 1 {z_n}}\overline{\partial}\varphi\circ(f\circ p)\in\cinf_{0,q+1}(\G).
  \end{align*}
  This is $\overline{\partial}$-closed. Thus there is $v\in\cinf_{0,q}$ such that $\overline{\partial}F=0$ and $F\circ j = f$.
  \par We now show that $\G_0$ satisfies the assumption in $\C^{n-1}$. Indeed if $f\in\cinf_{0,q}(\G_0)$ and $\overline{\partial}f=0$ extend $f$ to $F$ as before. There is $u\in\cinf_{0,q-1}(\G)$ with $\overline{\partial}u=F$. Take $v = u\circ j$, we then have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \overline{\partial}v = f.
  \end{equation*}
  For the induction hypothesis, there is $f\in\Ouv(\G_0)$ not extendable beyond $0$. As we have shown, $f$ extends to a holomorphic function $F$ on $\G$ and $F$ cannot be extended beyond $0$.
\end{proof}
Our next goal is to study $(\CR)_{0,q}$ solvability on pseudoconvex domains, namely 
\begin{equation*}
  H_{\Dol}^{p,q}(\G) = 0
\end{equation*}
for $q\geq 1$ and all $p$.
\section{Sheaves of cohomology}
The main reference is \cite{Hir}. \cite{Gr-Fr} also contains the exponsition with more details.
\subsection{Sheaves}

\begin{definition}
  Let $S,X$ be topological spaces. A pair $(\mathcal{S},\pi:\mathcal{S}\to X)$ is called a sheaf on $X$ if $\pi:\mathcal{S}\to X$ is a local homeomorphism. We will denote it simply by $\mathcal{S}$. 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
Let $U\subseteq X$ be an open subset. For a sheaf $\mathcal{S}$ on $X$. The section of $\mathcal{S}$ over $U$ is a continuous map $s:U\to \mathcal{S}$ such that $\pi\circ s = \id$. The stalk of $\mathcal{S}$ at $x\in X$ is the preimage $S_x = \pi^{-1}(x)$. 
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Fundamental Properties of Sheaves]Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a sheaf on $X$. 
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item For a section $s:U\to \mathcal{S}$, $s(U)$ is open. In particular, $\pi|_{s(U)}:s(U)\to U$ is a homeomorphism.
    \item $\{s(U)\}_{\substack{U\subset X\\\text{ open}}}$ forms a basis of the topology of $\mathcal{S}$. 
    \item $\mathcal{S}_x$ carries the discrete topology. 
    \item $\sigma\in \mathcal{S}_x$ then there is a neighborhood $U=U(x)$ of $x$ and a section $s:U\to \mathcal{S}$ such that $s(x) = \sigma$.
    \item Let $s,t$ be sections such that $s(x_0) = t(x_0)$. Then there is a neighborhood $U= U(x_0)$ such that $s\equiv t$ on $U$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
  A subsheaf $(\mathcal{S}',\pi')$ of a sheaf $\mathcal{S}\stackrel{\pi}{\to}X$ is a pair such that $\mathcal{S}'\subseteq\mathcal{S}$ is an open subset and $\pi' = \pi|_{\mathcal{S}}$.
  \par In particular, for a open subset $M\subseteq X$, $\pi^{-1}(M)$ is a subsheaf which is called a restriction of $\mathcal{S}$ to $M$.
\end{definition}

\begin{notation}
  We denote the set of all sections over $U$ as $\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})$. 
\end{notation}

\begin{remark}
  Clearly if $\mathcal{S}'\subseteq \mathcal{S}$ is a subsheaf we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma(U,\mathcal{S}')\subseteq\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S}).
  \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $(\mathcal{S},\pi_{\mathcal{S}}),(\mathcal{T},\pi_{\mathcal{T}})$ be sheaves on $X$. A morphism $\varphi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}$ of sheaves is a continuous map such that the following diagram commutes,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
\mathcal{S} \arrow[rd, "\pi_{\mathcal{S}}"'] \arrow[rr, "\varphi"] &   & \mathcal{T} \arrow[ld, "\pi_{\mathcal{T}}"] \\
                                                                   & X &                                            
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  This induces a map between stalks and for each $x\in X$, we denote,
  \begin{equation*}
    \varphi_x \defeq \varphi|_{\mathcal{S}_x}:\mathcal{S}_x\to\mathcal{T}_x.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  Given a morphism of sheaves, $\varphi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}$, this induces a map 
  \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})\ni s\mapsto\varphi\circ s\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{T}).
  \end{equation*}
  We will denote $\varphi|_U(s) = \varphi\circ s$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  For any morphism of sheaves $\varphi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}$, $\varphi(\mathcal{S})$ is a subsheaf of $\mathcal{T}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  A sheaf of abelian group is a sheaf $(\mathcal{S},\pi)$ on $X$ such that,
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item For each $x\in X$, $(\mathcal{S}_x,+)$ is an abelian group.
    \item For $(\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S}),+)$ is a group with group operation operation defined pointwise. That is 
    \begin{equation*}
      \Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})\times\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})(s,t)\mapsto s+t = [U\ni x\mapsto s(x)+t(x)]\in \Gamma(U,\mathcal{S}).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
  Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a sheaf of abelian groups. Then the map,
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}\fib{\pi}\mathcal{S} \stackrel{\pm}{\to}\mathcal{S},
  \end{equation*}
  is continuous where 
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}\fib{\pi}\mathcal{S} = \{(f,g)\in \mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\sep \pi(f) = \pi(g)\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
  $\mathcal{S}'\subseteq\mathcal{S}$ is a subsheaf of abelian groups if 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item it is a subsheaf of sets,
    \item $\mathcal{S}'_x$ is a subgroup of $\mathcal{S}_x$ for each $x\in X$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T}$ be sheaves of abelian groups. A sheaf homomorphism $\varphi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}$ is such that 
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $\varphi$ is a homomorphism of sheaves of sets,
    \item $\varphi_x:\mathcal{S}_x\to\mathcal{T}_x$ is a group homomorphism for each $x\in X$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  We have the following trivial statements.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item $\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})$ is an abelian group for any open subset $U\subseteq X$.
    \item $\varphi(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{T}$ is a subsheaf of $\mathcal{T}$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\varphi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}$ be a homomorphism of abelian sheaves. Then we define,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Ker\varphi = \bigsqcup_{x\in X}\{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_x\sep \varphi_x\sigma = 0\in\mathcal{T}_x\}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
  $\Ker\varphi$ is a subsheaf of $\mathcal{S}$. 
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
  Let $s:U\to\mathcal{S}$ be a section such that $s(x)\in\Ker\varphi$ for some $x\in X$. Then %later
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  For a sheaf of abelian group $\mathcal{S}$, the zero subsheaf is a subsheaf $\underline{0}$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \underline{0} = \bigsqcup_{x\in X}\{0_{\mathcal{S}_x}\}.
  \end{equation*}
  This is initial and final.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}
  For an open set $U\subseteq X$, $\varphi|_U:\Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})\to\Gamma(U,\mathcal{T})$ is a group homomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}
  A sheaf of commutative ring is a sheaf of abelian group $\mathcal{R}$ such that 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item For all $x\in X$, $\mathcal{R}_x$ is a commutative ring. 
    \item Multiplication is continuous that is for any open set $U\subseteq X$, 
    \begin{equation*}
      \Gamma(U,\mathcal{R})\times\Gamma(U,\mathcal{R})\ni(s,t)\mapsto st = [U\ni x\mapsto s(x)t(x)]
    \end{equation*}
    is a continuous map.
    \item There is a unit section $x\mapsto 1_x\in\mathcal{R}_x$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  $\Gamma(U,\mathcal{R})$ is a commutative ring for all $U\subset X$ open.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $R$ be a commutative ring and $X$ be a topological space. We define a constant sheaf $\underline{R}$ of $R$ over $X$ to be 
  \begin{equation*}
    X\times R\stackrel{\pi}{\to}X,
  \end{equation*}
  where $R$ is equipped with the discrete topology, $X\times R$ is equipped with product topology, and $\pi$ is a canonical projection.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  If $U\subseteq X$ is connected then $\Gamma(U,\underline{R})=R$.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}The following are example of sheaves of rings.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item $\Ouv$ a sheaf of germs of holomorphic functions. This is a Hausdorff space which can be proven using the identity theorem.
    \item For $k\in\N$, $\ck{k}(\C^n)$ is a sheaf of ring. Furthermore, we have a descending chain,
    \begin{equation*}
      \ck{0}(\C^n)\supseteq \cdots \supseteq \cinf(\C^n)\supseteq \Ouv.
    \end{equation*}
    \item The sheaf of meromorphic function $\mathcal{K}$.
    \item $\Ouv^*$ is the subsheaf of $\Ouv$ consisting of nowhere $0$ holomorphic functions and $\Ouv^*_x$ forms an abelian group under multiplication.
    \item $\mathcal{K}^*$ is defined similary.
  \end{enumerate}

\end{example}

  \begin{definition}
    Let $\mathcal{R}$ be a sheaf of commutative rings. We define a sheaf of $\mathcal{R}$-modules $\mathcal{A}$ to be such that 
    \begin{enumerate}[i).]
      \item it is a sheaf of abelian groups,
      \item $\mathcal{A}_x$ is an $\mathcal{R}_x$-module for each $x\in X$,
      \item for each open $U\subseteq X$, $\Gamma(U,\mathcal{R})\times \Gamma(U,\mathcal{A})\ni(r,a)\mapsto ra = [U\ni x\mapsto r(x)a(x)]$ is an element of $\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A})$ (i.e. defines a section over $U$ thus an action).
    \end{enumerate}
  \end{definition}

  \begin{definition}
    A presheaf $\mathscr{F}$ of sets is a contravariant functor on the category of open sets of $X$ with values in the category of sets. That is for two open sets $V\subseteq U\subseteq X$, recall that the inclusion relation is represented by an inclusion map $\iota:V\to U$. Since $\mathscr{F}$ is contravariant, this induces a morphism of set,
    \begin{equation*}
      \rho_{UV} \defeq \mathscr{F}(V\hookrightarrow U),
    \end{equation*}
    which we call a restriction map.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{definition}
    A homomorphism of presheaves is a functorial morphism (i.e. a natural transformation between two contravariant functors.) That is a collection of map $(\varphi_U)_U:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}$ such that the following diagram commutes for all $V\subseteq U$ open,
    \begin{center}
      \begin{tikzcd}
\mathcal{S}(U) \arrow[r, "\varphi_U"] \arrow[d, "\rho_{UV}^{\mathcal{S}}"'] & \mathcal{T}(U) \arrow[d, "\rho_{UV}^{\mathcal{T}}"] \\
\mathcal{S}(V) \arrow[r, "\varphi_V"']                                      & \mathcal{T}(V)                                     
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
  \end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathcal{S}\stackrel{\pi}{\to} X$ be a sheaf. The canonical presheaf associated $\mathcal{S}$ is a a functor $X\supseteq U\mapsto \Gamma(U,\mathcal{S})$.
\end{definition}


\begin{definition}
  A presheaf $\mathcal{S}$ is a sheaf if it is isomorphic to canonical presheaf of a sheaf.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
  Set $\mathcal{K}_x = \Frac(\Ouv_x)$ for each $x\in\C^n$ that is $\mathcal{K}_x$ is the germ of meromorphic functions. We define a presheaf $\mathcal{K}^{\pre}\defeq\bigsqcup_{x\in \C^n}\mathcal{K}_x\to\C^n$. The topology on $\mathcal{K}^{\pre}$ is define by the following way. Take an open subset $U\subseteq\C^n$ and sections $f,g\in\Gamma(U,\Ouv)$ such that $g$ is nowhere vanishing on $U$. Then the topology is generated by base element of the form $\left\{{\frac {f_x} {g_x}}\sep x\in U\right\}$. This does not define a sheaf.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a presheaf on $X$ and $x\in X$. Let $\mathcal{U}(x) = \{U\subseteq X\sep \text{$U$ is open and $x\in U$}\}$. Then we define,
  \begin{equation*}
  \tilde{\mathcal{S}}_x \defeq \bigsqcup_{U\in\mathcal{U}(x)} \mathcal{S}(U).
  \end{equation*}
  Let $f\in\mathcal{S}(U)$ and $g\in\mathcal{S}(V)$ for some open sets $U,V\subseteq X$. We define the equivalence relation $f\sim g$ if there is an open set $W\subseteq U\cap V$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \rho_{UW}(f) = \rho_{VW}(g).
  \end{equation*}
  The stalk of $\mathcal{S}$ at $x$ is then defined as,
  \begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{S}_x \defeq \tilde{\mathcal{S}}_x/\sim.
  \end{equation*}
  Furthermore, the sheafification of $\mathcal{S}$ is defined as,
  \begin{equation*}
  (\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\pi) \defeq\bigsqcup_{x\in X}\mathcal{S}_x \stackrel{\pi}{\to}X,
  \end{equation*}
  where $\mathcal{S}_x\ni(f,x)\stackrel{\pi}{\mapsto}x\in X$. We equip $\widehat{\mathcal{S}}$ with the topology by defining the base open sets as
  \begin{equation*}
    \forall U\subseteq X\text{ open}, s\in\mathcal{S}(U), \{\rho_{Ux}(f)=f(x)\sep x\in U\}.%later possible typo
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
  For any presheaf $\mathcal{S}$, $(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\pi)$ is a sheaf.
\end{proposition}

\begin{example}
  For any sheaf $\mathcal{S}$ we have $\mathcal{S}\cong \widehat{\mathcal{S}}$. 
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a sheaf on $X$. The presheaf of sections of $\mathcal{S}$ is $\Gamma_\mathcal{S}$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    X\supseteq U \mapsto \Gamma_{\mathcal{S}}(U) = \Gamma(U,\mathcal{S}).
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  \begin{equation*}
    \widehat{\Gamma_{\mathcal{S}}} \cong \mathcal{S}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
  $\widehat{(\cdot)}$ is a functor sending presheaves to sheaves. That is given a morphism of presheaves,
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}\stackrel{\varphi}{\to}\mathcal{T},
  \end{equation*}
  the sheafification then induces a morphism of sheaves,
  \begin{equation*}
    \widehat{\mathcal{S}}\stackrel{\widehat{\varphi}}{\to}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}.
  \end{equation*}
  Suppose $\mathcal{S}$ is a subpresheaf of $\mathcal{T}$ then 
  \begin{equation*}
    \widehat{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{T}}.
  \end{equation*}
  For a presheaf $\mathcal{S}$ and its sheafification $\widehat{\mathcal{S}}$, there is a canonical morphism 
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}\to\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}
  \end{equation*}
  By the first assertion, we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \widehat{\mathcal{S}}\to\widehat{\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
  The proposition above shows that we can justify denoting a sheaf $\mathcal{S}$ and its canonical presheaf $\Gamma_{\mathcal{S}}$ with the same letter.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a domain. The structure sheaf $\Ouv_\G$ is a sheaf of holomorphic functions on $\G$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  A ringed space is a pair $(X,\Ouv)$ such that 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item $X$ is a topological space, 
    \item $\Ouv\subseteq\mathcal{C}^0(X)$ a subsheaf of rings of continuous function.
  \end{enumerate}
  Given two ringed spaces $(X,\Ouv),(Y,\mathcal{R})$, a morphism of ringed spaces $(X,\Ouv)\stackrel{f}{\to}(Y,\mathcal{R})$ is a map such that 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item $f:X\to Y$ is continuous,
    \item for any $U\subset Y$ open, and $s\in\mathcal{R}(U)$, we have $s\circ f\in\Ouv(f^{-1}(U))$. 
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  A $n$-dimensional compact manifold is a ringed space $(X,\Ouv_X)$ such that 
  \begin{enumerate}[i).]
    \item $X$ is Hausdorff,
    \item $\forall x\in X$, there is a neighborhood $U=U(x)$ and an isomorphism $\varphi:(U\Ouv_U)\to (V,\Ouv_V)$ where $(V,\Ouv_V)$ is an open set in $\C^n$ with $\Ouv_V$ a sheaf of germs of holomorphic functions on $V$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{T}$ be sheaves of abelian groups. We define the factor sheaf $\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S}$ to be such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \forall x\in X, (\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S})_x \defeq \mathcal{T}_x/\mathcal{S}_x,
  \end{equation*}
  and thus 
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S} \defeq \bigsqcup_{x\in X}(\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S})_x.
  \end{equation*}
  The topology on $\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S}$ then defined as follows. For $U\subseteq X$ and $s\in\mathcal{T}(U)$, the base open sets are of the form,
  \begin{equation*}
    \{s(x)|x\in U\}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  $\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S}$ with above construction is a sheaf. Verifying it is left as an exercise to the readers.
\end{remark}

Alternatively, we could define $\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S}$ to be the sheafification of the following presheaf,

\begin{equation*}
  X\supseteq U\mapsto (\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S})(U) = \mathcal{T}(U)/\mathcal{S}(U).
\end{equation*}

\begin{remark}
  There is a surjective homomorhpism,
  \begin{equation*}
    \cT\twoheadrightarrow \cT/\cS,
  \end{equation*}
  which is induced by 
  \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_{\cT}(U)\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{\cT}(U)/\Gamma_{\cS}(U).
  \end{equation*}
  We then have a canonical morphism,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_{\cT}(U)/\Gamma_{\cS}(U)\to (\cT/\cS)(U).
  \end{equation*}
  We would expect it to be isomorphism, however, it turnes out that the map is neither injective nor surjective.
\end{remark}

This leads to the following question. 
\par Given a surjective map $\alpha:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}$, is it true that the associated map $\Gamma(\alpha):\Gamma_\cT\to\Gamma_\cS$ is again surjective?
\par This question then leads us to the theory of cohomology. That is we want to study when these canonically associated morphisms turn out to be isomorphisms.

\begin{definition}
  Let $M$ be an analytical set of a domain $\G\subseteq\C^n$. We define the associated sheaf of ideal to be,
  \begin{equation*}
    \G\supseteq U\mapsto \mathcal{I}_M(U) = \{f\in\Ouv(U)\sep f|_{M\cap U} \equiv 0\}.
  \end{equation*}
  Clearly, $\cI_M\subseteq\Ouv_\G$ as sheaves of abelian groups. The associated quotient sheaf is then defined as,
  \begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{\Ouv_M}\defeq\Ouv_\G/\cI.
  \end{equation*}
  With this, we define a structure sheaf of $M$ as
  \begin{equation*}
    \Ouv_M\defeq \widetilde{\Ouv_M}|_M.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  The pair $(M,\Ouv_M)$ is a ringed space. Also we call $\widetilde{\Ouv_M}$ to be the sheaf of weakly holomorphic functions on $M$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  A complex space is a ringed space $(X,\Ouv_X)$ such that for all $x\in X$ there is a neighborhood $U$ of $x$ and an isomorphism $\varphi:(U,\Ouv_X|_U)\to (M,\Ouv_M)$
   where $M$ is an analytic set in some open set $V\subseteq\C^n$ and $\Ouv_M$ is its structure sheaf. A morphism of ringed spaces between complex spaces are called a holomorphic map.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
  Let $(M,\Ouv_M)$ be a complex space, then $(M,\widetilde{\Ouv_M})$ is realized as collections of weakly holomorphic functions, We note that $\widetilde{\Ouv_M}\supseteq \Ouv_M$. We can even prove that $(M,\widetilde{\Ouv_M})$ is a complex space but this is not a trivial statement.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
  Let $\hat{\Ouv}_M$ be the sheaf of germs of normal functions. This is not a complex space but we can study how it is associated to a complex space. This lecture will not talk about this association however, the readers can look into more details in \cite{Gr-Fr}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Cohomology}
Let $(X,\Ouv_X)$ be a ringed space and $\cA,\cB,\cC$ be $\Ouv_X$-modules.
\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathscr{U}=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}$ be an open covering of $X$, $\cA$ be a sheaf on $X$. A $q$-cochain in $\cA$ with respect to $\mathscr{U}$ is a map $a:I^q\to \cA$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    a(i_0,\cdots,i_q)\in\cA\left(U_{i_0\cdots i_q}=\bigcup_{l=0}^qU_{i_l}\right),
  \end{equation*}
  which is alternating in the indices, that is 
  \begin{equation*}
    a(i_0,\cdots,i_j,\cdots,i_k,\cdots,i_q) = - a(i_0,\cdots,i_k,\cdots,i_j,\cdots,i_q).
  \end{equation*}
  We can view $a$ as an element of $\prod_{(i_0,\cdots,i_q)\in I^q}\cA(U_{i_0\cdots i_q})$. We denote the set of all $q$-cochains as 
  \begin{equation*}
    \cC^q(\mathscr{U},\cA),
  \end{equation*}
  which is an abelian group and even an $\Ouv_X$-module.
\end{definition}

\begin{notation}
  Let $V\subset U$ be open and $a\in\cA(U)$ then we will denote,
  \begin{equation*}
    a|_V^U \defeq \rho_{UV}(a).
  \end{equation*}
\end{notation}

\begin{definition}
  A coboundary is an $\Ouv_X$-module homomorphism $\delta:\cC^q(\mathscr{U},\cA)\to \cC^{q+1}(\mathscr{U},\cA)$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \delta(a)(i_0,\cdots,i_q,i_{q+1}) = \sum_{j=0}^{q+1}(-1)^ja(i_0,\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\cdots,i_{q+1})|^{U_{i_0\cdots i_{j-1}i_{j+1}\cdots i_{q+1}}}_{U_{i_0}\cdots i_{q+1}}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
  Let $\delta$ be a coboundary then,
  \begin{equation*}
    \delta\circ\delta = 0.
  \end{equation*}
  \label{chain_complex_boundary}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  This can be shown with direct calculations. 
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
  A sequence 
  \begin{equation*}
  0\to \cC^0(\mathscr{U},\cA)\stackrel{\delta}{\to} \cC^1(\mathscr{U},\cA)\stackrel{\delta}{\to} \cC^0(\mathscr{U},\cA)\stackrel{\delta}{\to}\cdots
\end{equation*}
is called the cochain complex of $\cA$ with respect to $\mathscr{U}$. We will denote this by 
\begin{equation*}
  (\mathscr{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cA)).
\end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Given a cochain complex of $(\mathscr{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cA))$. The $q$-cocycle is 
  \begin{equation*}
    Z^q(\mathscr{U},\cA) \defeq \Ker\delta=\Ker(\cC^q(\mathscr{U},\cA)\to \cC^{q+1}(\mathscr{U},\cA)).
  \end{equation*}
  The $q$-coboundary is 
  \begin{equation*}
    B^q(\mathscr{U},\cA) \defeq \Image\delta=\Image(\cC^{q-1}(\mathscr{U},\cA)\to \cC^{q}(\mathscr{U},\cA)).
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  By Proposition \ref{chain_complex_boundary}, we have $B^q(\mathscr{U},\cA)\subseteq Z^q(\mathscr{U},cA)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  $q$-th cohomology of $\cA$ with respect to $\mathscr{U}$ is defined as 
  \begin{equation*}
    H^q(\mathscr{U},\cA) = Z^q(\mathscr{U},\cA)/B^q(\mathscr{U},\cA).
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
  $\mathcal{C}^q(\mathscr{U},\cdot),\mathscr{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cdot)$, and $H^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cdot)$ are functorial that is for given a $\Ouv_X$ homomorphism $\cA\stackrel{f}{\to}\cB$, this induces 
  \begin{align*}
    \mathcal{C}^q(\mathscr{U},\cA)& \stackrel{\mathcal{C}^q(\mathscr{U},f)}{\to}\mathcal{C}^q(\mathscr{U},\cB),\\
    \mathcal{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cA)& \stackrel{\mathscr{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},f)}{\to}\mathcal{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cB),\\
    H^q(\mathscr{U},\cA)& \stackrel{H^q(\mathscr{U},f)}{\to}H^q(\mathscr{U},\cB).
  \end{align*}
\end{proposition}

This notion can be further generalized from $\Ouv_X$ modules to $\Ouv_X$ presheaves. In particular, $H^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\cdot)$ is a covariant functor from a category of $\Ouv_X$ presheaves to $\Ouv_X$ modules.

Furthermore, we have the following commutative diagram,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
\mathcal{A} \arrow[d, "\alpha"] \arrow[r] & {\mathcal{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\mathcal{A})} \arrow[d, "\mathscr{C}^\bullet(\alpha)"] \arrow[r] & {H^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\mathcal{A})} \arrow[d, "H^\bullet(\alpha)"] \\
\mathcal{B} \arrow[r]                     & {\mathcal{C}^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\mathcal{B})} \arrow[r]                                          & {H^\bullet(\mathscr{U},\mathcal{B})}                               
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
\begin{definition}
  Let $X$ be a topological space and $\mathscr{U}=(U_i)_{i\in I},\mathscr{V}=(V_j)_{j\in J}$ be two covering. $\mathscr{V}$ is finer than $\mathscr{U}$ if for each $i\in I$ there is $j\in J$ such that $V_j\subseteq U_i$. 
  \par In that case $\mathscr{V}$ is called a refinement of $\mathscr{U}$. We furthere define a refinement map which is a map $\varphi:J\to I$ such that $V_{j}\subseteq U_{\varphi(j)}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\sU,\sV$ be open coverings of a topological space $X$ and $a\in\cC^q(\sU,\cA)$. Suppose $\sV$ is a refinement of $\sU$ with a refinement map $\varphi:J\to I$. We define,
  \begin{equation*}
    \varphi^qa(j_0,\cdots,j_q) = a(\varphi(j_0),\cdots,\varphi(j_q))|^{U_{\varphi(j_0) \cdots \varphi(i_q)}}_{V_{j_0 \cdots j_q}}.
  \end{equation*}
  This defines maps $\varphi^\bullet:\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cA)\to\cC^\bullet(\sV,\cA)$ and 
  \begin{equation*}
    h_\varphi^\bullet:H^\bullet(\sU,\cA)\to H^\bullet(\sV,\cA).
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
  $h_\varphi^\bullet$ does not depend on $\varphi$. 
  \label{lemma_refinement_cech_universal}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  If $\varphi,\psi$ are two refinement maps then $\varphi^\bullet,\psi^\bullet$ are homotopic. Details can be found in \cite{Hir}.
\end{proof}
Suppose $\cA$ is a sheaf and $\Gamma_\cA$ be its canonical presheaf. 
\begin{proposition}
  The map,
  \begin{equation*}
    h^1:H^1(\sU,\cA)\to H^1(\sV,\cA),
  \end{equation*}
  is injective.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Details can be found in \cite{Hom}.
\end{proof}
The above proposition can be rephrashed by the following corollary.
\begin{corollary}
  Suppose we are given $c\in B^1(\sU,\cA)$, a cocycle. Under a refinement map $\varphi$, we have $\varphi^1c\in B^1(\sV,\cA)$. In other words, if $\varphi^1c=\delta b$ for some $b\in\cC^0(\sV,\cA)$, then there is $\tilde{b}\in\cC^0(\sU,\cA)$ such that $\delta\tilde{b}=c$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{notation}
  Motivated by Lemma \ref{lemma_refinement_cech_universal}, we introduce the following notation.
  \begin{equation*}
    h^\bullet(\sU,\sV):H^\bullet(\sU,\cA)\to H^\bullet(\sV,\cA).
  \end{equation*}
\end{notation}

Obviously we have,
\begin{equation*}
  h^\bullet(\sU,\sU) = \id.
\end{equation*}

Suppose we have refinements $\mathscr{W}\leq \sV\leq \sU$, we have,
\begin{equation*}
  h^\bullet(\sU,\mathscr{W}) = h^\bullet(\sV,\mathscr{W})\circ h^\bullet(\sU,\sV).
\end{equation*}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\mathscr{S}$ be a set of open coverings of $X$ such that for any open covering $\sU$ of $X$, there is a refinement $\sV$ of $\sU$ contained in $\mathscr{S}$. We then define the inductive limit,
  \begin{equation*}
    H^\bullet(X,\mathcal{A}) = \varinjlim_{\substack{\sU\\\text{\makebox[0pt][c]{open covering}}}}H^\bullet(\sU,\cA),
  \end{equation*}
  as an $\Ouv_X$-module such that for each $\sU\in\mathscr{S}$, there is a well-defined morphism,
  \begin{equation*}
    h^\bullet(\sU):H^\bullet(\sU,\cA)\to H^\bullet(X,\mathcal{A}),
  \end{equation*}
  satisfying that for any refinement map $\varphi:\sV\to\sU$, we have a commutative diagram,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
{H^\bullet(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[rr, "\varphi^\bullet"] \arrow[rd, "h^\bullet(\sU)"'] &                    & {H^\bullet(\sV,\cA)} \arrow[ld, "h^\bullet(\sV)"] \\
                                                                                 & {H^\bullet(X,\cA)} &                                                  
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  % Each element $\gamma\in H^q(X,\cA)$ is represented by an element $c\in H^q(\sU,\cA)$ such that $\delta(c) = 0$, for some $\sU\in\mathscr{S}$ such that if $c'\in H^q(\sU',\cA)$ where $\sU'\in\mathscr{S}$ and $\delta(c')=0$ also represents $\gamma$ then 
  % there is a refinement $\sU''\in\mathscr{S}$ such that 
  Each element $\gamma\in H^q(X,\cA)$ is represented by the following equivalence relation. For each $\sU,\sU'\in\mathscr{S}$ and $c'\in Z^q(\sU,\cA),c'\in Z^q(\sU',\cA)$, $c\sim c'$ if there is a refinement $\sU''$ of $\sU$ and $\sU'$ and $b\in \cC^{q-1}(\sU'',\cA)$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \delta b = h(\sU,\sU'')c-h(\sU',\sU'')c'.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

We note that the map $H^\bullet(X,\cdot)$ is functorial. That is given $\alpha:\cA\to\cB, \beta:\cB\to\cC$, we have,
\begin{align*}
  &h^\bullet(\alpha):H^\bullet(X,\cA)\to H^\bullet(X,\cB),\\
  &h^\bullet(\beta\circ\alpha) = h^\bullet(\beta)\circ h^\bullet(\alpha).
\end{align*}

\begin{remark}
  \par If $\mathcal{A}$ is a sheaf then we have,
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{A}(X)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{A}) = H^0(X,\mathcal{A}).
\end{equation*}
\end{remark}
 
In the case of $\Ouv_X$-modules, $H^\bullet$ and all the other ones turn out to be $\Ouv_X$-modules.

\begin{remark}
  For $a\in\cC^0(\sU,\cA)$, we have $a(i_0)\in\cA(U_{i_0})$ and $\delta a=0$ that is $a(i_0)-a(i_1) = 0$ on $U_{i_0i_1}$.
\end{remark}
With this we may define a global section $\tilde{a}\in\cA(X)$ glued from open cover $\{U_i\}_{i\in I}$ and $(a_i\in U_i)_{i\in I}$ that is 
\begin{equation*}
  \tilde{a}|_{U_i}^{X} = a_i.
\end{equation*}
\begin{definition}
  Let $\cA,\cB$ be presheaves. We say 
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha:\cA\to\cB
  \end{equation*}
  is 
  $\begin{cases}
    \text{injective},\\
    \text{surjective}
  \end{cases}$
  if for any open set $U$ of $X$ the map $\alpha(U):\cA(U)\to\cB(U)$ is 
  $\begin{cases}
    \text{injective},\\
    \text{surjective}
  \end{cases}$
  We define the following presheaves.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item $\ker\alpha = [U\mapsto \ker\alpha(U)]$,
    \item $\image\alpha = [U\mapsto \image \alpha(U)]$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\cA,\cB$ be sheaves. A morphism $\alpha:\cA\to\cB$ is 
  $\begin{cases}
    \text{injective},\\
    \text{surjective}
  \end{cases}$ if for any $x\in X$, the map $\alpha_x:\cA_x\to\cB_x$ is 
  $\begin{cases}
    \text{injective},\\
    \text{surjective}.
  \end{cases}$
  We define $\ker\alpha,\image\alpha$ to be the sheaf with stalks
  \begin{equation*}
    (\ker\alpha)_x = \ker\alpha_x, (\image\alpha)_x = \image\alpha_x.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  Given $\cA\stackrel{\alpha}{\to}\cB$ a morphism of presheaves and its corresponding sheaf morphism $\hat{\cA}\stackrel{\hat{\alpha}}{\to}\hat{\cB}$. All the above properties of $\alpha$ is transfered to $\hat{\alpha}$. The question remaining is is it true the other way around. 
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
  If $\hat{\alpha}$ is injective then $\alpha$ is also injective.
  \label{proposition_gamma_left_exact}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
  $\hat{\alpha}$ being surjective does not imply $\alpha$ is surjective.
  \label{remark_gamma_left_exact}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  A sequence 
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA \arrow[r] & \cB \arrow[r] & \cC \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  is exact if for any point $x$ of $X$, the sequence 
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA_x \arrow[r] & \cB_x \arrow[r] & \cC_x \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  is exact.
\end{definition}

For an exact sequence of presheaves,
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA \arrow[r] & \cB \arrow[r] & \cC \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}.

  We have 
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & {\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r, "\cC^\bullet(\alpha)"] & {\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cB)} \arrow[r, "\cC^\bullet(\beta)"] & {\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}

  By algebra, we have a long exact sequence,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & {H^0(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r] & {H^0(\sU,\cB)} \arrow[r] & {H^0(\sU,\cC)} \arrow[lld, "\delta_0^*"] \\
            & {H^1(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r] & {H^1(\sU,\cB)} \arrow[r] & {H^1(\sU,\cC)} \arrow[lld, "\delta_1^*"] \\
            & {H^2(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r] & {\cdots}                         &                                         
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}

\begin{proposition}
  Proposition \ref{proposition_gamma_left_exact} and Remark \ref{remark_gamma_left_exact} can be restated as follows. 
  \par Given a exact sequence of sheaves
    \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & {\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r, "\cC^\bullet(\alpha)"] & {\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cB)} \arrow[r, "\cC^\bullet(\beta)"] & {\cC^\bullet(\sU,\cA)} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  We have a following exact sequence,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\cA} \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\cB} \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\cC}
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
\end{proposition}

Let us denote $\widetilde{\Gamma_{\cC}} = \Gamma_{\cA}/\Gamma_{\cB}$. Then we have an exact sequence.
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\cA} \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\cB} \arrow[r] & \widetilde{\Gamma_{\cC}}\arrow[r] & 0.
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}

  The question is do we have the following equalities, 
  \begin{equation*}
    H^q(X,\cC) = H^q(X,\Gamma_{\cC})=H^q(X,\widetilde{\Gamma_{\cC}}).
  \end{equation*}

  \begin{definition}
    A Hausdorff space is said to be paracompact if each open covering admits a locally finite refinement.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{theorem_zero_sheaf_cohomology}
    Let $X$ be a paracompact space and $\cA$ be a presheaf. Suppose $\hat{\cA}=0$ then we have $H^q(X,\cA)=0$ for all $q$.
  \end{theorem}

  \begin{proposition}
    Let $\cS$ be a presheaf and $X$ be a paracompact space. Then we have,
    \begin{equation*}
      H^q(X,\cS) = H^q(X,\hat{\cS}).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{proposition}

  \begin{proof}
    Let $\Gamma_{\hat{\cS}}$ be its canonical presheaf and $\tau:\cS\to\Gamma_{\hat{\cS}}$ be a caonnical homomorphism.
    \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \ker\tau \arrow[r]    & \cS \arrow[r]                & \image \tau \arrow[r]                   & 0 \\
0 \arrow[r] & \image \tau \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\hat{\cS}} \arrow[r] & \Gamma_{\hat{\cS}}/\image\tau \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    By definition, we have $\ker\tau$ is a $0$-sheaf, similarly for $\Gamma_{\hat{\cS}}/\image \tau$. By Theorem \ref{theorem_zero_sheaf_cohomology}, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
      H^q(X,\ker\tau) = 0,H^q(X,\Gamma_{\hat{\cS}}/\image\tau)=0.
    \end{equation*}
    That is we have the following isomorphisms,
    \begin{equation*}
      H^q(X,\cS)\stackrel{\sim}{\to}H^q(X,\image\tau), H^q(X,\image\tau)\stackrel{\sim}{\to}H^q(X,\widehat{\Gamma_{\hat{\cS}}}).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{proof}

  \begin{definition}
    Let $\sU$ be an open covering of a topological space $X$ and $\cA,\cB$ be sheaves of abelian groups.
    $\sU$ is called $\cA$-acyclic if it is globally finite and $H^q(U_{i_0,\cdots,i_p},\cA)=0$ for all $p\geq0$ and $q\geq1$.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{theorem}[Leray]
    If $\sU$ is acyclic for $\cA$, then $H^q(X,\cA)\cong H^q(\sU,\cA)$.
  \end{theorem}

  \begin{proof}
    See \cite{Hir}.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{definition}
    A sheaf of abelian group $\cA$ is acyclic if $H^q(X,\cA)=0$ for all $q\geq1$.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{definition}
    An acyclic resolution of a sheaf of abelian groups $\cA$ is a long exact sequence,
    \begin{center}
      \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA \arrow[r, "h"] & \cA^0 \arrow[r, "h^0"] & \cA^1 \arrow[r, "h^1"] & \cA^2 \arrow[r] & {\cdots}
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    such that each $\cA^p$ is acyclic for $p\geq0$.
  \end{definition}

  Now consider,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA(X) \arrow[r, "h_X"] & \cA^0(X) \arrow[r, "h^0_X"] & \cA^1(X) \arrow[r, "h^1_X"] & \cA^2(X) \arrow[r] & {\cdots}
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}

  This is no longer an exact sequence. Thus we consider a cohomology of $\cA^0(X)$.

  \begin{theorem}
    Consider an acyclic resolution,
    \begin{center}
      \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA \arrow[r, "h"] & \cA^0 \arrow[r, "h^0"] & \cA^1 \arrow[r, "h^1"] & \cA^2 \arrow[r] & {\cdots}
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    We have 
    \begin{equation*}
      H^q(X,\cA) = H^q(\cA^0(X)).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{theorem}

  \begin{proof}
    For $q=0$, we have ,
    \begin{equation*}
      H^0(X,\cA)=\cA(X) = \ker h^0_X = \ker[\cA^0(X)\to\cA^1(X)] = H^0(\cA^0(X)).
    \end{equation*}
    Denote $\overline{\cA} = \image h^0\subseteq\cA^1$. Now,
    \begin{center}
      \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \cA \arrow[r] & \cA^0 \arrow[d, "h^0"] \arrow[r, "h^0"]         & \cA^1 \arrow[r] & {} \\
            &               & \overline{\cA} \arrow[ru, "i"', hook] \arrow[d] &                 &    \\
            & 0 \arrow[ru]  & 0                                               &                 &   
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    We then have,
    \begin{center}
      \begin{tikzcd}
(I) & 0 \arrow[r] & \cA \arrow[r]            & \cA^0 \arrow[r] & \overline{\cA} \arrow[r] & 0      \\
(II) & 0 \arrow[r] & \overline{\cA} \arrow[r] & \cA^1 \arrow[r] & \cA^2 \arrow[r]          & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    THus we have,
    \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
\cA^0(X) \arrow[r] & \overline{\cA}(X) \arrow[r] & {H^1(X,\cA)} \arrow[r] & {\underbrace{H^1(X,\cA^0)}_{=0}}
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    This means that 
    \begin{equation*}
      H^1(X,\cA) = \overline{\cA}(X)/h^0(\cA^0(X)) = {\frac {\ker\{h^1:\cA^1(X)\to\cA^2(X)\}} {h^0(\cA(X))}} = H^1(\cA^0(X)).
    \end{equation*}
    For $q:,1\leq q\leq \tilde{q}$, we set, $\cB=\cA^{q+1}$. We then have another acyclic resolution of $\overline{\cA}$.
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \overline{\cA} \arrow[r] & \cB^0 \arrow[r] & \cB^1 \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
Thus we obtain $H^i(\cB^\bullet(X)) = H^{q+1}(\cA^\bullet(X))$. Therefore,
\begin{equation*}
  H^{q+1}(X,\cA) \cong H^q(X,\overline{\cA}).
\end{equation*}
By induction on the exact cohomology sequence, we have,
\begin{equation*}
  H^q(X,\overline{\cA})=H^q(\cB^\bullet(X))\cong H^{q+1}(X,\cA^\bullet(X)).
\end{equation*}
  \end{proof}
  \subsection{DeRham and Dolbeault Theorems}
  
\begin{theorem}
  Let $X$ be a smooth manifold (ie. $\G\subseteq\C^n$). Let $\cA$ be a sheaf of germs of $\cinf(X)$. Then $\cA$ is acyclic that is $H^q(X,\cA)=0$ for $q\geq1$.
\end{theorem}
\setcounter{claim}{0}
\begin{proof}
  Let $\sU$ be an open covering of $X$ with infinite index set $I$. 
  \begin{claim}
  \begin{equation*}
    q\geq1\Rightarrow H^q(\sU,\cA) = 0.
  \end{equation*}
  \end{claim}
  \begin{claimproof}
  Let $(\varphi_i)_{i\in I}$ be a smooth partition of unity subordinates to $\sU$. Let $c\in\cC^q(\sU,\cA)$ be such that $\delta(c)=0$.
  We now define 
  \begin{equation*}
    b(i_0,\cdots,i_{q-1}) = \sum_{i\in I}\varphi_i c(i,i_0,\cdots,i_{q-1}).
  \end{equation*}
  Then we have,
  \begin{align*}
    \delta b & = \sum_{i\in I}\sum (-1)^jc(i,i_0,\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\cdots,i_q).\\
    (\delta b)(i_0,\cdots,i_q) &= \sum_{i\in I}\varphi_i\sum_{j}(-1)^jc(i,\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = \sum_{i\in I} \varphi_i c(i_0,\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = \underbrace{\left(\sum_{i\in I}\varphi_i\right)}_{=1}c(i_0,\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = c(i_0,\cdots,i_q).
  \end{align*}
  \end{claimproof}
  \par The statement follows from the claim.
\end{proof}
%Current current
\begin{remark}
  Let $\cE^p$ be a smooth $p$-forms on $X$. Then they are acyclic.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
  Let $\G\subseteq\R^N$ be a convex domain and $f\in\cinf_q(\G)$, with $df=0$. Then $f=du$ for some $u\in\cinf_{q-1}(\G)$.
\end{theorem}

We have an exact sequence,
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \C \arrow[r] & \cE^0 \arrow[r, "d"] & \cE^1 \arrow[r, "d"] & \cE^2 \arrow[r, "d"] & \cdots \arrow[r, "d"] & \cE^N \arrow[r, "d"] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}

This is an acyclic resolution of $\C$. Then we have, for $X$ connected
\begin{equation*}
  H^q(X,\C) \cong H^q(\cE^\bullet(X)) = {\frac {\{\text{$d$-closed $q$-forms}\}} {\{\text{$d$-exact $q$-forms}\}}} = H_{\dR}^q(X).
\end{equation*}

\begin{theorem}[de Rham]
  \begin{equation*}
    H^q(X,\C) \cong H^q_{\dR}(X).
  \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
  \begin{equation*}
    H^q(X,\C) \cong H^q_{\sing}(X),
  \end{equation*}
  where the left hand side is a sheaf cohomology and the right hand side is from topology.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $X$ be a compact manifold (eg. $\G\subseteq\C^n$). We define $\Omega^p(X)$ to be a sheaf of germs of holomorphic $p$-forms.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  $\Omega^0(X)=\Ouv(X)$ a structure sheaf.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  For each $p$, we have an acyclic resolution,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Omega^p \arrow[r] & {\cE^{p,0}} \arrow[r, "\overline{\partial}"] & {\cE^{p,1}} \arrow[r, "\overline{\partial}"] & {\cE^{p,2}} \arrow[r, "\overline{\partial}"] & \cdots \arrow[r, "\overline{\partial}"] & {\cE^{p,n}} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Dolbeault]
  \begin{equation*}
    H^q(X,\Omega^p)\cong H^{p,q}(\cE^{p,\bullet}(X)) = H^{p,q}_{\Dol}(X).
  \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
  For $p=0$, we have $\Omega^0(X)=\Ouv(X)$. Thus we hae,
  \begin{equation*}
    H^q(X,\Ouv) \cong H^{0,q}_{\Dol}(X).
  \end{equation*}
  That is for all $f\in\cE^{0,q}(X)$, if $\overline{\partial}f=0$ there is $u\in\cE^{0,q-1}(X)$ with $\overline{\partial}u = f$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
  Let $\mathcal{E}_{pq}$ be smooth forms on $\G\subseteq\C^n$. Then $H^p(\G,\mathcal{E}_{pq})=0$ for $q\geq1$. 
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Let $\sU$ be a locally finite open covering with index set $I$. Let $(\varphi_i)_{i\in I}$ be a partition of unity subordinates to $\sU$. 
  Let $f\in Z^q(\sU,\mathcal{E})$, then 
  \begin{equation*}
    a(i_0,\cdots,i_{q-1}) \defeq \sum_{i\in I}\varphi_if(i,i_0,\cdots,i_{q-1}).
  \end{equation*}
  Then 
  \begin{align*}
    (\delta a)(i_0,\cdots,i_q) & = \sum_{j=0}^q(-1)^ja(i_0,\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = \sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j=0}^q(-1)^j\varphi_if(i,i_0,\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = \sum_{i\in I}\varphi_i\sum_{j=0}^q (-1)^j f(i,i_0,\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j+1},\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = -\left(\sum_{i\in I}\varphi_i\right) f(i_0,\cdots,i_q),\\
    & = -f.
  \end{align*}
  Therefore $f$ is also exact. For $q=1$, we have, $f(i_0,i_1)$, 
  \begin{equation*}
    a(i_0) = \sum_{\varphi_i}f(i,i_0), a(i_1) = \sum\varphi_if(i,i_1). 
  \end{equation*}
  Therefore,
  \begin{equation*}
    (\delta a)(i_0,i_1) = a(i_1)-a(i_0) = \sum\varphi_i(f(i,i_1)-f(i,i_0)).
  \end{equation*}
  The readers are encouraged to do the case when $q=1$ manually to see make sense of this proof.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Global Problems}

We will start with the first cousin problem. Using the terminologies we have introduced, we can rewrite the problem as follows.
\par Let $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{K}/\Ouv$. Then we have the exact sequene,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv \arrow[r, hook] & \mathcal{K} \arrow[r, two heads] & \mathcal{H} \arrow[r] & 0.
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Is it true that we have,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv(X) \arrow[r, hook] & \mathcal{K}(X) \arrow[r, two heads] & \mathcal{H}(X) \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
still exaft?
\begin{theorem}
  If $H^1(X,\Ouv)=0$ then the first Cousin problem is solvable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  We have a cohomology sequence,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv(X) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}(X) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{H}(X) \arrow[r] & {H^1(X,\Ouv)} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  Thus the assumption directly shows the statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  For any polydisk $X$, we have $H^1(X,\Ouv)=0$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  We define the sheaf of germs of divisors to be $\mathcal{D} = \mathcal{K}^*/\Ouv^*$. 
\end{definition}

Recall the second cousin problem, stating that is all divisors principle. We have an exact sequence,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv^* \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}^* \arrow[r] & \mathcal{D} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv^*(X) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}^*(X) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{D}(X) \arrow[r] & {H^1(X,\Ouv^*)} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  Consider the map $\Ouv\ni \mapsto \exp(2\pi if)\in\Ouv^*$. The kernel of this map is $\Z$ thus induces a following exact sequence,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Z \arrow[r] & \Ouv \arrow[r, "\substack{f\mapsto\exp(2\pi i f)\\\:}"] & \Ouv^* \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  From this we get a cohomology sequence,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
\cdots \arrow[r] & {H^1(X,\Ouv)} \arrow[r] & {H^1(X,\Ouv^*)} \arrow[r] & {H^2(X,\Z)} \arrow[r] & {H^2(X,\Ouv)} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  If we have $H^1(X,\Ouv),H^2(X,\Ouv)=0$ then $H^1(X,\Ouv^*)\cong H^2(X,\Z)$. Then for $H^2(X,\Z)=0$, that is all divisors are principal.
  \begin{remark}
  The above argument can be further generalized. if $D$ is a domain then we have a following map.
  \begin{equation*}
  D\mapsto \underbrace{\tilde{D}}_{\in H^1(X,\Ouv^*)}\mapsto \underbrace{c(D)}_{\in H^2(X,\Z)}.
  \end{equation*}
  Such $c(D)$ is called the Chern class of $D$.
\end{remark}
  \begin{theorem}
    If $H^1(X,\Ouv),H^2(X,\Ouv)=0$, then precisely the divisors with $c(D)=0$ are principal. 
  \end{theorem}

  \begin{remark}
    For example, above conditions are attained when $X$ is a polydisk.
  \end{remark}

  Now we will examine extorsion problems. Let $M$ be an analytic set on $X$ and $\mathcal{I}_M$ be the corresponding ideal sheaf of $M$. Then we have,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{I}_M \arrow[r] & \Ouv \arrow[r] & \Ouv_M = \Ouv/\mathcal{I}_M \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}

The question is, is the sequence,
\begin{tikzcd}
\Ouv(X) \arrow[r] & \Ouv_M(X) \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
exact?

\begin{theorem}
  If $H^1(X,\mathcal{I}_M)=0$, then holomorphic functions on $M$ are restrictions of holomorphic functions on $X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  From the sequence,\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{I}_M \arrow[r] & \Ouv \arrow[r] & \Ouv_M = \Ouv/\mathcal{I}_M \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center},
  we get a cohomology sequene,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
\cdots \arrow[r] & \Ouv(X) \arrow[r] & \Ouv_M(X) \arrow[r] & {H^1(X,\mathcal{I}_M)} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
\end{proof}

Let $x\in\C^n$ and define and $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a domain containing the origin. We then define 
\begin{equation*}
  J_{0,x} = \begin{cases}
    \Ouv_x,\quad x\not=0,\\
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}

We then obtain the following exact sequence,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K} \arrow[r] & \mathcal{O}^n \arrow[r, "{\substack{(f_1,\cdots,f_n)\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^nf_jz_j\\\:}}"] & \mathcal{J}_0 \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}

The question is for $f\in\Ouv(\mathcal{G})$ with $f(0)=0$, does there exists $f_j\in\Ouv(\G)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
\sum f_jz_j = f.
\end{equation*}

This turns out to be true if $H^1(\G,\mathcal{K})=0$. Furthermore if $H^q(\G,\Ouv)=0$ for $q\geq1$, we also get the answer to be positive.


\section{Vanishing of Dolbeault Spaces}

Now we turn our interest to solving Cauchy-Riemann equations.

\begin{theorem}
  If $\G$ is pseudoconvex then $(\CR)_q$ is solvable for $q\geq1$. That is for all $f\in\cinf_{0,q}(\G)$, $\overline{\partial}f=0$ implies that there is $u\in\cinf_{0,q-1}(\G)$ such that $\overline{\partial}u = f$.
\end{theorem}

This section studies the general solutions of these equations.

\subsection{Spaces of forms}

Through out this subsection, we will denote $\G\subseteq\C^n$ as a domain and $E$ be some function spaces. We will denote, 
\begin{notation}
  $E_{pq}$ will denote a $(p,q)$-forms with coefficient in $E$. That is 
  \begin{equation*}
    f = \sum_{\substack{\vert I\vert=p\\\vert J\vert q}}f_{I,J}dz^Id\overline{z}^J,
  \end{equation*}
  where $I\in\{1,\cdots,n\}^p,J\in\{1,\cdots,n\}^q$. 
\end{notation}

\begin{remark}
  For $I\in\{1,\cdots,n\}^p$, we will denote $I'$ to be the tuple where $I'_1<\cdots<I'_p$. Then we denote
  \begin{equation*}
    f_{IJ} = \varepsilon_{I'J'}^{IJ} f_{I'J'},
  \end{equation*}
  where $\varepsilon_{I'J'}^{IJ}$ is the product of signs of permutations which send $I\to I',J\to J'$. Under this notation, we get
  \begin{equation*}
    f = {\frac 1 {p!q!}}\sum f_{IJ}dz^Id\overline{z}^J.
  \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  For one dimensional case $\R$, in order to define a distribution. Let us first consider the set of test functions, that is 
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{D} \defeq \cinf_C(\R).
  \end{equation*}
  A sequence $(\varphi_n)_{n\in \N}$ in $\mathcal{D}$ converges to $0$ in $\mathcal{D}$ if $(\varphi_n)_{n\in \N}$ converges uniformly to $0$ and $\supp \varphi_n$ is contained in a finite measure interval for any $n\in\N$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition} 
  A distribution is a linear continuous map $f:\mathcal{D}\to\R$ where we consider. Thus the space of distributions of $\mathcal{D}$ is precisely the continuous dual $\mathcal{D}'$ of $\mathcal{D}$.
  The topology on $\mathcal{D}'$ is defined as follows. $(f_n)_{n\in \N}$ converges to $0$ in $\mathcal{D}'$ if $f_n(t)\to 0$ for each $t\in \mathcal{D}$. 
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
  Any locally integrable function defines a continuous dual. Let $f$ be a locally integrable funciton. Then we define,
  \begin{equation*}
    \tilde{f}[t] = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(x)t(x)dx.
  \end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
  The Dirac $\delta$-distribution at the origin is defined as.
  \begin{equation*}
    \delta[f] = f(0).
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  Such $\delta$ is not induced by any function.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
  Let $t\in\mathcal{D},f\in\mathcal{D}$ then we define, 
  \begin{equation*}
    f'[t] = -f[t'].
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

We refer to \cite{laurent} for more details about distributions.

\begin{theorem}
  Let $(f_n)_{n\in \N}$ be a sequence in $\mathcal{D}'$ then 
  \begin{equation*}
    f_n\stackrel{\mathcal{D}'}{\to}f\Rightarrow f_n'\stackrel{\mathcal{D}'}{\to}f'.
  \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
  Let us take $E=\mathcal{D}$ the set of test functions (more generally, we can take $\mathcal{D}'$ the space of distributions). We define a bilinear form for $f,q\in \mathcal{D}'_{p,q}$ by 
  \begin{equation*}
    \langle f,g\rangle(z) = \sum f_{IJ}(z)\overline{g}_{IJ}(z).
  \end{equation*}
  Let $\varphi$ be a continuous function which is everywhere positive on $\G$. We then define a scalar product,
  \begin{equation*}
    (f,g)_\varphi \defeq \int_\G \langle f,g\rangle(z)e^{-\varphi(z)}d\lambda(z).
  \end{equation*}
  We will denote,
  \begin{equation*}
    \norm{f}_\varphi^2 = (f,f)_\varphi.
  \end{equation*}
  We call $(\cdot,\cdot)_\varphi$ a scalar product with respect to the weight function $\varphi$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  We denote $L^2_{p,q,\loc}(\G)$ to be the set of locally square integrable functions/forms on $\G$. We also define,
  \begin{equation*}
    L^2_{p,q}(\G,\varphi) = \{f\in \mathcal{D}'_{p,q}\sep \norm{f}_\varphi<\infty\}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  $(L^2_{p,q}(\G,\varphi),(\cdot,\cdot)_\varphi)$ is a Hilbert space and we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi)\subseteq L^2_{p,q,\loc}(\G).
  \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
  $\mathcal{D}_{p,q}$ is dense in $L^2_{p,q}(\G,\varphi)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{lemma}
  Let $f\in L_{p,q,\loc}^2(\G)$, then there is a weight function $\varphi$, such that $f\in L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Choose $\varphi_0$ such that $1\in L^2(\G,\varphi_0)$. That is 
  \begin{equation*}
    \int_\G e^{-\varphi(z)}\lambda(z)<\infty.
  \end{equation*}
  Let us take $\varphi$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \varphi\geq \varphi_0+\log\vert f\vert^2.
  \end{equation*}
  Then 
  \begin{equation*}
    \norm{f}_{\varphi}^2 = \int_\G\vert f\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda \leq \int_\G\vert f\vert^2e^{-\varphi_0-\log\vert f\vert^2}d\lambda\leq \norm{1}_{\varphi_0}<\infty.
  \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

  Let $f\in L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi)$ then $\overline{\partial}f\in\mathcal{D}_{p,q+1}(\G,\varphi)$. Let $\varphi$ be a weight such that
  \begin{equation*}
    L^2_{p,q+1}(\G,\varphi)\subseteq \mathcal{D}'_{p,q+1}.
  \end{equation*}
  If $\overline{\partial}f\in L_{p,q+1}^2(\G,\varphi)$, then we define,
  \begin{equation*}
    T:L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi)\to L^2_{p,q+1}(\G,\varphi), f\mapsto\overline{\partial}f.
  \end{equation*}
  We have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Dom T = \{f\sep \overline{\partial}f=Tf\in L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi)\}\subseteq \mathcal{D}_{p,q}.
  \end{equation*}

 $T$ is densely defined.

 \begin{lemma}
  Such $T$ is a closed operator that is the graph $\Gamma_T\subseteq L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi)\times L_{p,q+1}^2(\G,\varphi)$ is closed.
 \end{lemma}

 \begin{proof}
  Let $(f_n)_{n\in \N}$ be a sequence in $\Dom T$ such that $(f_n,Tf_n)\stackrel{L^2}{\to}(f,g)$. We want to show that $f\in\Dom T$ and $g = Tf$.
  Observe that $L^2$-convergence implies $\mathcal{D}'$ convergence. Therefore, 
  \begin{equation*}
    Tf_n = \overline{\partial}f_n \to \overline{\partial}f.
  \end{equation*}
  Therefore, we conclude $f\in \Dom T, g=\overline{\partial}f = Tf$. 
 \end{proof}

 \begin{proposition}
  $T$ is closed densely defined linear operator between Hilbert spaces.
 \end{proposition}

 \begin{definition}
    We define the null space $\mathcal{N}_T$ of $T$ to be
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{N}_T \defeq \{x\in\Dom T\sep Tx = 0\}.
    \end{equation*}
    And the range $\mathcal{R}_T$ of $T$ to be 
    \begin{equation*}
      \mathcal{R}_T\defeq \{Tx\sep x\in\Dom T\}.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}

  \begin{remark}
    $\mathcal{N}_T$ is closed but $\mathcal{R}_T$ is not necessarily closed.
  \end{remark}


  Let $\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3$ be weight functions. Consider
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
{L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi_1)} \arrow[r, "T"] & {L_{p,q+1}^2(\G,\varphi_2)} \arrow[r, "S"] & {L_{p,q+2}^2(\G,\varphi_3)}
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  where $T,S$ are both defined by $\overline{\partial}$.
  We have $\mathcal{N}_S$ is closed, and $\overline{\partial}\circ\overline{\partial}=0$, therefore 
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{R}_T\subseteq\mathcal{N}_S.
  \end{equation*}

The natural question to ask is when do we have an equality to the above inclusion. This question leads us to find general solutions of $(\CR)_{p,q+1}$.
\subsection{Unbounded Operators in Hilbert Spaces}
We first recall the definition of linear operators between Hilbert spaces.
\begin{definition}
  Let $H_1,H_2$ be Hilbert spaces. A linear operator is a linear map $T:H_1\to H_2$ which is defined on some subspace $\Dom T$ of $H_1$. (Thus $T$ need not be defined in the whole space.) Similarly as before, $\mathcal{R}_T$ is defined as the range of $T$ which is a subspace of $H_2$. 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
  A linear operator $T:H_1\to H_2$ is densely defined if $\Dom T$ is dense in $H_1$. $T$ is closed if the graph,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_T \defeq \{(f,Tf)\sep f\in \Dom T\}
  \end{equation*}
  is closed in $H_1\times H_2$.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
  If $T:H_1\to H_2$ is closed then $\mathcal{N}_T$ is closed in $H_1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proposition}
  Suppose $T:(H_1,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_1)\to (H_2,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_2)$ be a linear operator which is densely defined. Let $x\in\Dom T,y\in H_2$. Let us define,
  \begin{equation*}
    l_y(x)\to \langle Tx,y\rangle_2.
  \end{equation*}
  If $l_y$ is bounded then $l_y$ extends to $H_1$. Using Riesz representation theorem, we obtain a linear map $T^*:H_2\to H_1$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    l_y(x) = \langle Tx,y\rangle_2 = \langle x,T^*y\rangle_1.
  \end{equation*}
  We have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Dom T^* = \{y\in H_2\sep \exists c \st \vert\langle Tx,y\rangle_2\vert\leq c\norm{x}_1\}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
  Let $T:H_1\to H_2$ be a linear operator. The adjoint operator $T^*:H_2\to H1$ of $T$ is a linear operator such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \langle Tx,y\rangle_2 = \langle x,T^*y\rangle.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
  Let $T:H_1\to H_2$ be a densely defined closed linear operator. Then its adjoint $T^*:H_2\to H_1$ is also densely defined and closed. Moreover $T^{**}=T$.
  Furthermore, in this case, we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    H_1 = \mathcal{N}_T\oplus \overline{\mathcal{R}_{T^*}},H_2 = \mathcal{N}_{T^*}\oplus \overline{\mathcal{R}_{T}}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
  Left as an exercise to the readers.
\end{proof}
The question we would like to study is what are the conditions for 
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{R}_T = \overline{\mathcal{R}_T},\mathcal{R}_{T^*} = \overline{\mathcal{R}_{T^*}},
\end{equation*}
that is when the range of either $T$ or $T^*$ closed?
\begin{theorem}
  Let $T:(H_1,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_1)\to(H_2,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_2)$ be a densely defined closed operator such that $\mathcal{R}_T\subseteq F$ for some closed subspace $F$. We have the following are equivalent.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item $\mathcal{R}_T=F$,
    \item $\exists c>0$ such that for all $y\in\Dom T^*\cap F$,
    \begin{equation*}
      \norm{y}_2\leq C\norm{T^*y}_1.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{enumerate}
  If $\mathcal{R}_T$ is closed then we have,
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \setcounter{enumi}{2}
    \item $T^*$ has its range closed.
    \item $\forall x\in\mathcal{R}_{T^*}$ there is $y\in\Dom T^*$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
      \norm{y}_2\leq c\norm{x}_1.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{enumerate}
  \label{theorem_closedness_range}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  The complete proof can be found in \cite{Hom} 4.1.1 and 4.1.2.
\end{proof}
\subsection{The Basic Estimates}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a domain and $\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3\in\cinf_c(\G)$ be everywhere positive test functions. Consider the sequence 
    \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
{L_{p,q}^2(\G,\varphi_1)} \arrow[r, "T"] & {L_{p,q+1}^2(\G,\varphi_2)} \arrow[r, "S"] & {L_{p,q+2}^2(\G,\varphi_3)}
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  We say the basic estimate holds for the sequence if there is a constant $C>0$ such that for all $f\in\Dom T^*\cap\Dom S$, we have
  \begin{equation*}
    \norm{f}_{\varphi_2}^2\leq C^2(\norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_3}^2).
  \end{equation*}
  \label{definition_basic_estimate}
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
  Suppose the basic estimate holds, then for $f\in\mathcal{N}_S$, we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \norm{f}^2_{\varphi_2}\leq C^2\norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2.
  \end{equation*}
  \label{proposition_basic_estimate_kernel}
\end{proposition}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{theorem_solution_cr_be}
  If the basic estimate holds then $\mathcal{R}_T = \mathcal{N}_S$. That is for any $f\in\mathcal{N}_S$ there is $u\in L_{p,q}^2(\varphi_1)$ such that $Tu=f$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Follows from Proposition \ref{proposition_basic_estimate_kernel} together with Theorem \ref{theorem_closedness_range}.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  Theorem \ref{theorem_solution_cr_be} tells us that this solves $(\CR)_{p,q+1}$ in the sense of distributions.
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
  In the case of constants, Definition \ref{definition_basic_estimate} says,
  for $a,b,c>0$ we have,
  \begin{align*}
    c\leq a+b&\Rightarrow c^2\leq 2(a^2+b^2),\\
    c^2\leq a^2+b^2&\Rightarrow c\leq a+b.
  \end{align*}
\end{remark}
We now move on to find conditions for the basic estimate. Let $\varphi,\psi$ be everywhere positive test functions. Set,
\begin{align*}
  \varphi_1 & = \varphi-2\psi,\\
  \varphi_2 & = \varphi-\psi,\\
  \varphi_3 & = \varphi
\end{align*}
Let us impose some conditions on $\psi$. Consider a chain of smooth functions
\begin{equation*}
  \eta_1\leq\eta_2\leq\cdots,
\end{equation*}
such that $\eta_i\in[0,1]$ for each $i\in\N$ and for any compact set $K$, there is $i\in\N$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  K\subseteq \{\eta\equiv 1\}.
\end{equation*}
\begin{definition}
  $\psi$ satisfies the following equation,
  \begin{equation*}
    \vert \partial\eta_n\vert^2\leq e^\psi,\tag{H}\label{equation_h_cond}
  \end{equation*}
  then we say $\psi$ satisfies \eqref{equation_h_cond}.
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
  \eqref{equation_h_cond} can be fulfilled.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Let $K$ be a compact subset of $\G$. For $\psi$ to satisfy finitely many (construction)? Suppose $K\subset K'$ is also compact then $\psi$ can be extended without changing on $K$ to satisfy \eqref{equation_h_cond}. %needs revision.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
  $\varphi,\psi$ be given. If the equation,
  \begin{equation*}
    L_\varphi(z;t)\geq 2(\vert\partial\psi(z)\vert^2+e^{\psi(z)})\norm{t}^2,\tag{E}\label{equation_e_cond}
  \end{equation*}
  where $L_\varphi$ is the Levi form is satisfied, we say $\varphi$ satisfies condition \eqref{equation_e_cond} with respect to $\psi$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
  If $\varphi$ satisfies \eqref{equation_e_cond}, then it is necessarily strictly plurisubharmonic. Furthermore, it admits plurisubharmonic smooth exhaustive functions. Therefore $\G$ is necessarily pseudoconvex. This follows from Theorem \ref{theorem_pseudo_convex_equiv}
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}[Hörmander]
  Let $\varphi,\psi$ be such that together they satisfy \eqref{equation_h_cond} and \eqref{equation_e_cond}. Let us put,
  \begin{align*}
  \varphi_1 & = \varphi-2\psi,\\
  \varphi_2 & = \varphi-\psi,\\
  \varphi_3 & = \varphi.
  \label{equation_d_cond}\tag{D}
\end{align*}
Then the basic estimates hold with constant $C=1$. That is we have,
\begin{equation*}
\norm{f}^2_{\varphi_2}\leq \norm{T^*f}^2_{\varphi_1}+\norm{Sf}^2_{\varphi_3}.
\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{theorem}[Friedrich/Hörmander]
  Suppose $\psi$ satisfies \eqref{equation_h_cond} and $\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3$ are given as in Equation \eqref{equation_d_cond}. Then $\mathcal{D}_{p,q+1}$ is dense in $\Dom T^*\cap \Dom S$.
  \par Moreover, $f\in\Dom T^*\cap \Dom S$ then there is a sequence $(f_j)_{j\in\N}$ in $\mathcal{D}_{p,q+1}$ such that 
  \begin{align*}
    f_j&\stackrel{L^2}{\to}f,\\
    \overline{\partial}f_j&\stackrel{L^2}{\to}Sf,\\
    T^*j&\stackrel{L^2}{\to}T^*f.\\
  \end{align*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  The complete proof can be found in \cite{Hom} 4.1.3.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  Let $\psi,\varphi$ satisfy \eqref{equation_h_cond} and \eqref{equation_e_cond}. Suppose $\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3$ are given as in Equation \eqref{equation_d_cond}. Then, for each $f\in\mathcal{D}_{p,q+1}$, we have 
  \begin{equation*}
  \norm{f}^2_{\varphi_2}\leq \norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_3}.
  \end{equation*}
  That is, it satisfies the basic estimate.
\end{theorem}
\subsection{Proof of Basic Estimate formel}
Since $\C^n$ admits a global coordinate therefore we can assume $p=0$ without the loss of genearlity.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
{(\mathcal{D}_{0,q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\varphi_1})} \arrow[r, "T=\overline{\partial}", shift left] & {(\mathcal{D}_{0,q+1},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\varphi_2})} \arrow[l, "T^*", shift left]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Compute $T^*$ explicitly that is, for $fin\mathcal{D}_{0,q+1}$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  f = \sum_{\vert J\vert = q+1}f_J d\overline{z}^J.
\end{equation*}
Then consider,
\begin{equation*}
  u = \sum_{\vert K\vert = q}u_Kd\overline{z}^K\in\mathcal{D}_{0,q}.
\end{equation*}
We want 
\begin{equation*}
  (T^*f,u)_{\varphi_1} = (f,\overline{\partial}u)_{\varphi_2}.
\end{equation*}
Write,
\begin{equation*}
  T^*f = \sum_{\vert K\vert = q}(T^*f)_Kd\overline{z}^K.
\end{equation*}
Note that we have,
\begin{equation*}
  \overline{\partial}u = \sum_K\sum_{j=1}^n \pd{u_K}{\overline{z}_j}d\overline{z}_j\wedge d\overline{z}^K.
\end{equation*}
Using the equation above, we have,
\begin{align*}
  (T^*f,u)_{\varphi_1} & =\int\sum_K (T^*f)_K\overline{u}_Ke^{-\varphi_1}d\lambda. \\
  (f,\overline{\partial}u) & = \int\sum_K\sum_{j=1}^n \pd{u_K}{\overline{z}_j}f_{(j,K)}d\overline{z}_j\wedge d\overline{z}^Ke^{-\varphi_2}d\lambda,\\
  & = -\int\sum_{K}\sum_j \pd{(f_{(j,K)}e^{-\varphi_1})}{z_j}\overline{u}_Ke^{\varphi_1}e^{-\varphi_2}d\lambda.
\end{align*}
Compute $T^*f = e^{\varphi_1}\sum_{K,j}\pd{(f_{(j,K)}e^{-\varphi_1})}{z_j}d\overline{z}^K$. 
We set,
\begin{equation*}
  e^{\varphi_1}(\theta(e^{-\varphi_1}f)) \defeq e^{\varphi_1}\sum_{K,j}\pd{(f_{(j,K)}e^{-\varphi_1})}{z_j}d\overline{z}^K.
\end{equation*}
Compute $\vert \overline{\partial}f\vert^2$. Note we have,
\begin{equation*}
  \overline{\partial}f=\sum_{\vert J\vert = q+1}\pd{f_j}{\overline{z}_j}d\overline{z}_j\wedge d\overline{z}^J.
\end{equation*}
Therefore,
% \begin{equation*}
%   \vert \overline{\partial}f\vert^2 = \langle\overline{\partial}f,\overline{\partial}f\rangle   = \sum_{\vert J\vert = q+1}\sum_j \left\vert \pd{f_j}{z_j}\right\vert^2 - \sum_{\vert K\vert = q}\sum_{j\not=k}\pd{f_{(j,K)}}{\overline{z}_k}\overline{\pd{f_{(k,K)}}{z_j}}.
% \end{equation*}
\begin{align*}
  \vert\overline{\partial}f\vert^2 & = \langle\overline{\partial}f,\overline{\partial}f\rangle,\\
  & = \sum_{\vert J\vert = q+1}\sum_{\vert L\vert =q+1}\sum_j\sum_l\pd{f_J}{\overline{z}_j}\overline{\pd{f_L}{\overline{z}_l}}\cdot\varepsilon^{jJ}_{lL}.
\end{align*}
where,
\begin{equation*}
  \varepsilon_{lL}^{jJ}=\begin{cases}
    0,\quad j\in J,\\
    0,\quad l\in L,\\
    0,\quad (j,J)\not=(l,L)\text{ as sets},\\
    \sgn{jJ\choose lL}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Thus decompose into two signs, where $j=l$ or $\varepsilon_{lL}^{jJ}=1$. 
For the first case,
\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{\substack{j,J}} \left\vert\pd{f_j}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert^2. 
\end{equation*}
For $j\not=l$, we have $j\in L$ and $l\in J$, set $L\backslash\{j\}=J\backslash\{l\}\defeq K$. Then,
\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{J,L}\sum_{j\not=l}\pd{f_{J}}{\overline{z}_j}\overline{\pd{f_{L}}{\overline{z}_l}}\varepsilon_{lL}^jJ.
\end{equation*}
Observe that we have,
\begin{equation*}
  \varepsilon_{lL}^{jJ}=\varepsilon_{jlK}^{jJ}\varepsilon_{ljK}^{jlK}\varepsilon_{lL}^{ljK} = -\varepsilon_{jlK}^{jJ}\varepsilon_{lL}^{ljK}=-\varepsilon_{lK}^J\varepsilon_L^{jK}=-1.
\end{equation*}
Thus,
\begin{equation*}
  -\sum_{\vert K\vert q}\sum_{j\not=l}\pd{f_{lK}}{\overline{z}_j}\overline{\pd{f_{jK}}{\overline{z}_l}}.
\end{equation*}
Combining two cases together we obtain,
\begin{equation*}
  \vert \overline{\partial}f\vert^2 = \sum_{J,j}\left\vert\pd{f_J}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert-\sum_{\vert K\vert =q}\sum_{j,k}\pd{f_{jK}}{\overline{z}_k}\overline{\pd{f_{kK}}{\overline{z}_j}}.
\end{equation*}
Choose $\varphi,\psi>0$ be smooth. Choose $\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3$ be such that 
  \begin{align*}
  \varphi_1 & = \varphi-2\psi,\\
  \varphi_2 & = \varphi-\psi,\tag{D}\\
  \varphi_3 & = \varphi.
\end{align*}
And set $\psi$ so that it satisfies \eqref{equation_h_cond}, that is there is a sequence $(\eta_n)_{n\in\N}$ such that $\eta_n(\G)\subseteq[0,1]$, and for any compact set there is $n\in \N$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  K\subseteq \{\eta_n=1\}.
\end{equation*}
And $\psi$ satisfies 
\begin{equation*}
  \vert \partial\eta_n\vert^2\leq e^\psi\tag{H}.
\end{equation*}
Let $v$ be a function on $\G$ and $\varphi$ be a weight function,
\begin{align*}
  \delta_j v& = e^\varphi\pd{}{z_j}(e^{-\varphi}v),\\
  & = \pd{v}{z_j}-\pd{\varphi}{z_j}v.
\end{align*}
Insert $\delta$ in $T^*f$ to obtain,
\begin{align*}
  e^\varphi T^*f = -\sum_{\vert K\vert =q}\left(\sum_j \delta_jf_{jK}+\pd{\psi}{z_j}f_{jK}\right)d\overline{z}^K.
\end{align*}
Now, we compute the following scalar product. All integrals are now over $\C^n$ and have compact support in $\G$.
\begin{align*}
  \int \sum_{K}\sum_{j,k}\delta_jf_{jK}\overline{\delta_kf_{kK}}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda& = \int \left\vert e^\psi T^*f+\sum\pd{\psi}{z_j}f_{jK}\right\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda,\\
  & \leq 2\int e^{2\psi}\vert T^*f\vert^2 e^{-\varphi}d\lambda+2\int \vert\partial\psi\vert^2\vert f\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda
\end{align*}
Now add $\int\vert\partial f\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda$ to both sides and thi yields,
\begin{align*}\label{equation_star}
  \int\sum_K\left(\sum_{j,k}\delta_jf_{jK}\overline{\delta_kf_{kK}}-\pd{f_{jK}}{\overline{z}_k}\overline{\pd{f_{kK}}{\overline{z}_j}}\right)e^{-\varphi}d\lambda &+ \int \sum_J\sum_j\left\vert\pd{f_J}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert e^{-\varphi}d\lambda,\tag{$\ast$}\\
  &\leq \norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_2}^2+2\int\vert\partial\psi\vert^2\vert f\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda.
\end{align*}
\begin{lemma}
  Let $v_1,v_2\in\mathcal{D}_0$ we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \int v_1\overline{\pd{}{\overline{z}_k}v_2}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda = -\int\delta_kv_1\overline{v_2}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda.
  \end{equation*}
  Moreover, we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \delta_j\pd{}{\overline{z}_k}-\pd{}{\overline{z}_k}\delta_j={\frac {\partial^2\varphi}{\partial z_j\partial\overline{z}_k}}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
Using the lemma above, Equation \eqref{equation_star}, becomes 
\begin{align*}
  -\int\sum_{K}\sum_{j,k}\left(\pd{}{\overline{z}_k}\delta_jf_{jK}-\delta_j\pd{f_{jK}}{\overline{z}_k}\right)\overline{f}_{kK}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda=\int\sum_{K}\sum_{j,k}{\frac {\partial^2\varphi}{\partial z_j\partial\overline{z}_k}}f_{jK}\overline{f_{kK}}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda.
\end{align*}
Now this leads us to 
\begin{align*}\label{equation_double_star}
\int\sum_{K}\sum_{j,k}{\frac {\partial^2\varphi} {\partial z_j\partial\overline{z}_k}}f_{jK}\overline{f}_{kK}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda&+\int\sum_J\sum_j\left\vert\pd{f_j}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda \tag{$\ast\ast$}\\
&\leq 2\norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_3}^2+2\int\vert f\vert^2\vert\partial\psi\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda
\end{align*}
In particular for $q=0$, we have,
\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{\vert J\vert = q+1}\left\vert\pd{f_j}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert^2\leq\norm{T^*f}^2+\norm{Sf}^2.
\end{equation*}
Let $\varphi$ be strictly plurisubharmonic, then 
\begin{equation*}
  L_\varphi(z;t)\geq c(z)\norm{t}^2,
\end{equation*}
