where $c$ is a positive function on $\G$.
\begin{equation*}
  \int(c(z)-\vert\partial\psi(z)\vert^2)\vert f\vert^2 e^{-\varphi}d\lambda\leq 2\norm{T^*f}^2_{\varphi_1}+\norm{Sf}^2_{\varphi_3}.\tag{$\ast\ast\ast$}\label{equation_triple_star}
\end{equation*}
Finally introduce the condition \eqref{equation_e_cond} on $c$, that is 
\begin{equation*}
c(z)>2(\vert\partial \psi(z)\vert^2+e^{\psi(z)})\Leftrightarrow c(z)-2\vert\partial\psi\vert^2>2e^{\psi}.
\end{equation*}
Then Equation \eqref{equation_triple_star} is now,
\begin{equation*}
  2\norm{f}_{\varphi_2}^2\leq 2\norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_3}^2.
\end{equation*}
Thus we conclude,
\begin{equation*}
  \norm{f}_{\varphi_2}^2\leq \norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_3}^2.
\end{equation*}
\begin{proposition}
  Suppose $\varphi,\psi$ together satisfy conditions \eqref{equation_h_cond},\eqref{equation_d_cond},\eqref{equation_e_cond}, and the triplet $(\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3)$ is defined as in \eqref{equation_d_cond}, then the basic estimate holds in $\mathcal{D}_{0,q}$. Namely,
  \begin{equation*}
  \norm{f}_{\varphi_2}^2\leq \norm{T^*f}_{\varphi_1}^2+\norm{Sf}_{\varphi_3}^2.
\end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{theorem}
  \eqref{equation_h_cond},\eqref{equation_d_cond},\eqref{equation_e_cond} yield the basic estimate in $\Dom T^*\cap\Dom S$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{remark}
  The theorem tells us that $\G$ must be pseudoconvex.
\end{remark}
\subsection{Solving $(\CR)$}
\begin{theorem}
  Let $f\in L^2_{0,q+1,\loc}(\G)$ where $\G$ is pseudoconvex. Suppose 
  \begin{equation*}
  \overline{\partial}f=0.
  \end{equation*}
  Then there is $u\in L^2_{0,q,\loc}(\G)$ with 
  \begin{equation*}
    \overline{\partial}u = f.
  \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Will be shown in the coming lecture on this friday. Roughly, we will show there are $(\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3)$ such that $f\in L^{0,q,\loc}(\G,\varphi_2)$ and the basic estimate holds. By the previous subsection, we know $f\in\mathcal{R}_T$ that is $f=\overline{\partial}u$ for some $u$.
  Let $p$ be strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion function such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    L_p(z;t)\geq m(z)\norm{t},
  \end{equation*}
  is continuous positive function. Let $\chi:\R\to\R$ be such that 
  \begin{align*}
    s\geq0&\Rightarrow\chi(s)\equiv 0,\\
    s>0&\Rightarrow\chi(s)>0,\chi'(s)>0,\chi''(s)>0\
  \end{align*}
  We then set $\varphi=\chi\circ p$. Then,
  \begin{equation*}
    L_\varphi(z;t)\geq \chi'(p(z))m(z)\norm{t}^2.
  \end{equation*}
  Choose $\psi$ at to satisfy \eqref{equation_h_cond}. To satisfy \eqref{equation_e_cond}, we need,
  \begin{equation*}
  L_\varphi(z;t)\geq \chi'(p(z))m(z)\norm{t}^2\geq(\vert\partial\psi\vert^2+e^\psi)\norm{t}^2.
  \end{equation*}
  Note that
  \begin{equation*}
    \chi(p(z))m(z)\geq\vert\partial \psi\vert^2+e^{\psi(z)},\chi'(p(t))\geq{\frac {\vert\partial \psi\vert^2+e^{\psi(z)}}{m(z)}}.
  \end{equation*}
  We then consider the following condition.
  \begin{equation*}
    \chi'(s)\geq\sup_{p(z)\leq s}{\frac {\vert \partial \psi(s)\vert^2+e^{\psi(z)}}{m(z)}}. \tag{$E'$}\label{equation_e_prime_condition}
  \end{equation*}
  We then have \eqref{equation_e_prime_condition} implies \eqref{equation_e_cond}. $\chi'$ can be  found that $\chi''>0,\chi>0$.
  We cann still enlarge $\varphi$ and free $p$ \eqref{equation_h_cond},\eqref{equation,equation_d_cond},\eqref{equation_e_cond}. Thus 
  \par $f$ given ($\overline{\partial}f=0$), there are $\varphi,\psi$ so that $f\in L_{0,q+1}^2(\G,\varphi_2)$ where $\varphi_2=\varphi-\psi$. 
  Thus the basic estimate holds and $f\in\mathcal{R}_T$ by the previous subsection(be more specific). Thus there is $u\in L_{0,q}^2(\G,\varphi_1)$ with $\overline{\partial}u=f$.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
Let $s=0,1,\cdots$. Then 
\begin{equation*}
  W^s \defeq \{f\in L^2(\C^n)\sep\text{all derivatives of order at most $s$ are in $L^2(\C^n)$}\}.
\end{equation*}
And 
\begin{equation*}
  W^s_{\loc}(\G)\defeq \left\{f\sep\substack{\text{All derivatives of $f$ of order less than $s$ are in $L^2(K)$}\\\text{ where $K$ runs through all compacts sets of $\G$}}\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Furthermore,
\begin{equation*}
  W^s_{0,q,\loc}(\G)
\end{equation*}
be $(0,q)$-forms with coefficients in $W^s_{\loc}(\G)$. Note that $W_{0,q,\loc}^0(\G)=L_{0,q,\loc}^2(\G)$.
\end{definition}
\begin{theorem}
  \begin{equation*}
    W^{s+2n}_{\loc}(\G)\subseteq \mathcal{C}^s(\G).
  \end{equation*}
  where $\G\subseteq\C^n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  See any functional analysis book.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
  Consider
  \begin{align*}
    f & = \sum_{\vert J\vert = q+1}f_Jd\overline{z}^J,\\
  \end{align*}
  then we define,
  \begin{equation*}
  \vartheta f = \sum_{\vert K\vert = q}\left(\sum_j\pd{f_j}{z_j}\right)d\overline{z}^K.
  \end{equation*}
  Note that $\vartheta:\mathcal{D}_{0,q+1}'\to\mathcal{D}_{0,q}'$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
  Check that $\vartheta\vartheta=0$. (Exercise)
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
  A function $\chi:\C^n\to\R$ is called radial is for any $z,z'\in\C^n$, 
  \begin{equation*}
    \norm{z}=\norm{z'}\Rightarrow \chi(z) = \chi(z').
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
  \label{lemma_regularity_1}
  Let $f\in L^2_{0,q+1}(\G)$ with compact support. Suppose $\overline{\partial}f$ and $\vartheta f$ belong to $L^2$. Then $f\in W_{0,q+1,\loc}^1(\G)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Let $f\in\mathcal{D}_{0,q+1}$. Recall \eqref{equation_double_star}, 
  \begin{equation*}
    \int\sum_{\vert J\vert=q+1}\sum_j \left\vert\pd{f_j}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert d\lambda\leq(\norm{\overline{\partial}f}^2+\norm{\vartheta f}^2).
  \end{equation*}
  So the $\overline{z}$-derivatives are uniformly estimated. 
  \par In general regular regularise. That is, we consider a smooth radial function $\chi$ 
  \begin{equation*}
    \chi(z)\geq0,\int_{\C^n}\chi(z)d\lambda(z)=1.
  \end{equation*}
  Set,
  \begin{equation*}
    \chi_{\varepsilon}(z) = \left({\frac 1 {\varepsilon^{2n}}}\right)\chi\left({\frac z \varepsilon}\right).
  \end{equation*}
  Then we set,
  \begin{equation*}
    f_\varepsilon(z) \defeq f\ast\chi_\varepsilon(z) = \int f(\zeta)\chi_\varepsilon(\zeta-z)d\lambda(\zeta) = \int f(z-\varepsilon\zeta)\chi(\zeta)d\lambda(\zeta).
  \end{equation*}
  Then $f_\varepsilon$ is smooth and approximate $f$ in $L^2$-norm uniformly and furthermore, the convergence commutes with $\overline{\partial}$. 
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}
  \label{lemma_regularity_theta_dbar}
  Let $f\in L_{0,0}^2,\overline{\partial}f\in L_{0,1}^2$ with compact support. Then we also have $\partial f\in L_{1,0}^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Assume $f\in\mathcal{D}_{0,0}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \int\left\vert \pd{f}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert d\lambda(z) = -\int{\frac {\partial^2 f}{\partial\overline{z}_j\partial z_j}}\overline{f}(z)d\lambda(z)=\int\left\vert\pd{f}{z_j}\right\vert^2d\lambda(z).
  \end{equation*}
  FOr the general case, regularize as above.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  Let $\G$ be pseudoconvex. Let $f\in W^s_{0,q+1,\loc}(\G)$ with $\overline{\partial}f=0$. Then there is $u\in W^{s+1}_{0,q,\loc}(\G)$ with $\overline{\partial}u = f$.
  For $q=0$, all solutions are $W^{s+1}_{0,q,\loc}(\G)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  For $q=0$, we have some solution $\overline{\partial}u=f$. And $u\in L^2_{0,q,\loc}(\G)=W^0_{0,q}(\G)$. Choose a smooth function $\chi$ with compact support. Then we examine, $\chi u$. 
  \par Assume $u\in W^\sigma$ whre $0\leq \sigma\leq s$. We already now that $u$ belongs to $\sigma=0$. Then consider,
  \begin{equation*}
    \pd{}{\overline{z}_j}\chi u = \pd{\chi}{\overline{z}_j}u+\chi\pd{u}{\overline{z}_j}\in W^\sigma.
  \end{equation*}
  Let $D$ be a differential operator with constant coefficient of order $\sigma$. Then,
  \begin{equation*}
    \pd{}{\overline{z}_j}D\chi u = D\underbrace{\pd{}{\overline{z}_j}\chi u}_{\in W^{\sigma}}\in W^{0}
  \end{equation*}
  By Lemma \ref{lemma_regularity_theta_dbar}, $\pd{}{z_j}D\chi u\in W^0$. Thus $\chi u\in W^{\sigma+1}$ Therefore, $u\in W^{\sigma+1}_{\log}(\G)$.
  \par For $q>0$. We use $H_1\stackrel{T,T^*}{\leftrightarrow}H_2$. 
  We know,
  \begin{equation*}
    H_1 = \mathcal{N}_T\oplus\mathcal{R}_{T^*},H_2 = \mathcal{N}_{T^*}\oplus\mathcal{R}_T.
  \end{equation*}
  Let $f\in\mathcal{R}_T$ then there is $u\in H_1$ such that $Tu=f$. Set 
  \begin{equation*}
    u = u_0+u_1,
  \end{equation*}
  where $Tu_1=f$ and $u_0\in\mathcal{N}_T$. Thus $u\in\mathcal{R}_{T^*}$ therefore, there is $v$ such that $u_1=T^*v$.  We then have two equations,
  \begin{equation*}
    Tu_1 = f,u_1 = T^*v.
  \end{equation*}
  In our case, we want to find $u$ with 
  \begin{equation*}
    \overline{\partial}u = f,u=T^*v.
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{align*}
    \vartheta(e^{-\varphi_1}u) & = \vartheta(e^{-\varphi_1}T^*v),\\
    & = \vartheta(e^{-\varphi_1}e^{\varphi_1}\vartheta(e^{-\varphi_2}v)),\\
    & = \vartheta\circ\vartheta(e^{-\varphi_2}v)=0.\\
    \vartheta(e^{-\varphi_1}u) & = e^{-\varphi_1}\vartheta u+Au.
  \end{align*}
  where $A$ is a differential operator of order $0$. Let $f\in W^s$ assume $u\in W^\sigma$ for some $0\leq \sigma\leq s$. 
  We will show that $u\in W^{\sigma+1}$. Let $\chi$ be as above case when $q=0$.
  \begin{align*}
    \overline{\partial}(\chi u)& = \overline{\partial}\chi au + \chi\overline{\partial}u\in W^\sigma,\\
    \vartheta(\chi u) & = \chi\vartheta u +bu = \chi au+bu\in W^\sigma.
  \end{align*} 
  Continue as for $q=0$, and for a differential operator $D$ of order $\sigma$, we have,
  \begin{align*}
    \overline{\partial}D(\chi u) &= D\overline{\partial}\chi u\in W^0,\\
    \vartheta D(\chi u) & = D\vartheta \chi u \in W^0.
  \end{align*}
  By Lemma \ref{lemma_regularity_1}, we have $D\chi u\in W^1$ thus $\chi u\in W^{\sigma+1}$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  $\G$ is pseudoconvex and $f\in\cinf_{0,q+1}(\G)$ with $\overline{\partial}f=0$.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item for $q=0$, each solution $\overline{\partial}u=f$ is smooth,
    \item for $q>0$, there is some $u\in \cinf_{0,q}$ with $\overline{\partial}u=f$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  $f\in W^s_{\loc}(\G)$ for all $s>2n$. Find $u\in W^{s+1}_{\loc}(\G)$. FoR $u\in W^{s+1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{s+1-2n}$. $u$ is uniformly differentiable and $u\in\cinf_{0,q}(\G)$. 
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  $\overline{\partial}$ is an elliptic operator on functions. $\overline{\partial}$ is not elliptic for $q>0$. In that case a smooth solutions will be singled out by an adjoint operator ($T^*$ essentially $\theta$), $\overline{\partial}\oplus\theta$ is elliptic.
\end{remark}
\subsection{Applications}

\begin{definition}
  Let $f\in \cinf_{p,0}(\G)$ with $\cbd f=0$. The $p$-form is holomorphic it can be written as
  \begin{equation*}
    f = \sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\\vert\alpha\vert = p}}f_\alpha dz^\alpha,
  \end{equation*}
  where $f_\alpha$ are all holomorphic. We write,
  \begin{equation*}
    \Omega^p = \{\text{germs of holomorphic $p$-forms}\}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  \begin{equation*}
    \Omega^0=\Ouv,\Omega^p \cong \Ouv^{{n\choose p}}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
  For $\G$ a domain in $\C^n$, the following are equivalent.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item $\G$ is pseudoconvex.
    \item $\G$ is holomorphically convex.
    \item $\G$ is a domain of holomorphy.
    \item $\G$ is a domain of existence.
    \item $H^{p,q}_{\Dol}(\G)=0$ for all $q\geq1$.
    \item $H^q(\G,\Omega^p)=0$ for all $q\geq1$, and solves the Levi problems.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}
  The first Cousin problem is solvable in a domain of holomorphy. Namely,
  \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{O} \arrow[r] & \mathcal{M} \arrow[r]     & \mathcal{K}=\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{O} \arrow[r] & 0                           &        \\
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv(\G) \arrow[r]    & \mathcal{M}(\G) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}(\G) \arrow[r]                     & {H^1(\G,\Ouv)=0?} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
\end{theorem}

Consider the second Cousin problem. That is let $\G$ be a domain and $\sU=\{U_i\sep i\in I\}$ be an open covering. Consider,
\begin{equation*}
  f_i\in\mathcal{M}^*(U_i), f_i/f_j = h_{ji}\in\Ouv^*(U_{ij}).
\end{equation*}

Cousin II distribution ion is a solution to the second Cousin problem which is a element $f\in\mathcal{M}^*(\G)$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
  f/f_i = h_i\in\Ouv^*(U_i).
\end{equation*}

Consider,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Ouv^* \arrow[r] & \mathcal{M}^* \arrow[r] & \mathcal{D}=\mathcal{M}^*/\mathcal{O}^* \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Note that $\mathcal{D}(\G)$ is a group of divisors. Consider for $f\in\mathcal{M}^*(\G)$, the representative $(f)$ in $\mathcal{D}$ is called a principal divisor. (i.e. the Cousin II distribution solvable?).
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
\cdots \arrow[r] & \mathcal{M}^*(\G) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{D}(\G) \arrow[r] & {H^1(\G,\Ouv^*)} \arrow[r] & {\underbrace{H^1(\G,\mathcal{M}^*)}_{=0}} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Since $\G$ is pseudoconvex, we have $\mathcal{D}(\G)\to H^1(\G,\Ouv^*)=0$. 
\par We also have what so-called exponential sequence,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \Z \arrow[r] & \Ouv \arrow[r, "\substack{f\mapsto \exp(2\pi if)\\\:}"] & \Ouv^* \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
This gives a rise to a cohomology sequence,
\begin{center}
  \begin{tikzcd}
\cdots \arrow[r] & {H^1(\G,\Ouv)} \arrow[r] & {H^1(\G,\Ouv^*)} \arrow[r] & {H^2(\G,\Z)} \arrow[r] & {H^2(\G,\Ouv)} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Imposing the assumption that $\G$ is pseudoconvex, we get 
\begin{equation*}
  H^1(\G,\Ouv^*)\cong H^2(\G,\Z).
\end{equation*}
From these observation, we have the following theorem.
\begin{theorem}
  The Second Cousin problem on a pseudoconvex domain $\G$ is solvable if and only if $H^2(\G,\Z)=0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{theorem}[Oka-Grauert Principle]
  On a pseudoconvex domain, problems that can be expressed in terms of cohomology are solvable if and only if they admit continuous solutions. Or equivalently, the obstructions to solving these problems come from the topology of the domains.
\end{theorem}

\begin{example}
  We give a counterexample to the Second Cousin problem where $\G$ pseudoconvex and simply connected. Set,
  \begin{equation*}
    \G = \{\vert z_1^2+z_2^2+z_3^2-1\vert<1\}\compembed\C^3.
  \end{equation*}
  Then $\G$ is simply connected but $H^2(\G,\Z)=\Z$. The idea to show this equality is the following. Observe first that 
  \begin{equation*}
    \G\cong D_1(0)\times Q,
  \end{equation*}
  where $Q = \{z_1^2+z_2^2+z_3^2=1\}$. Note that $Q$ can be retracted to $S^2$. Thus the second cohomology of $Q$ is the same as $S^2$ which is $\Z$. But the unit disk is contractible therefore, we obtain the claim.
\end{example}
\begin{theorem}[Poincaré Problem]
  Let $\G$ be a pseudoconvex domain and $H^2(\G,\Z)=0$ If $f\in\mathcal{M}(\G)$ then there exist $g,h\in\Ouv(\G)$ such that $f=g/h$.
\end{theorem}
  Let $D\in\mathcal{D}(\G)$. Then it is locally given by the Cousin II distribution. Explicitlly, for $\sU=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}$ an open covering such that $f_i\in\mathcal{M}(U_i)$ is meromorphic and it is given by $f_i = {\frac {g_i} {h_i}}$. We can assume that 
  $g_{i,x},h_{i,x}$ are relatively prime for certain point $x$. On $U_{ij}=U_i\cap U_j$, there is $u_{ij}$ such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    f_i=u_{ij}f_j.
  \end{equation*}
  where $u_{ij}$ is a holomorphic function without zeros. This tells us that there exist $u^g_{ij},u^h_{ij}$ such that on $U_{ij}$, we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    g_i = u^g_{ij}g_j,h_i = u^h_{ij}h_j,
  \end{equation*}
  and they are nowhere zero holomorphic functions. Note that collections,
  \begin{equation*}
    \{(U_i,g_i)\sep\text{Cousin II}\}\stackrel{\text{defines}}{\to}D^+,\{(U_i,h_i)\sep\text{Cousin II}\}\stackrel{\text{defines}}{\to}D^-.
  \end{equation*}

\begin{definition}
  The divisor $D$ is positive if it is in the image of 
  \begin{equation*}
    \tilde{\Ouv}\to\mathcal{M}^*,
  \end{equation*}
  where $\tilde{\Ouv}_x = \Ouv_x-\{0\}$.
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
  Let $D\in\mathcal{D}(\G)$. Then there exists $D^+,D^-$ such that $D^+$ is a positive divisor and 
  \begin{equation*}
    D = D^+-D^-.
  \end{equation*}
  Furthermore, this decomposition is unique. In particular, $D,D^+,D^-$ are principle. That is $D=(f)$ then for $f=u{\frac g h}$ where $u\in\Ouv^*(\G)$, we have ,
  \begin{equation*}
    (f)=(g)-(h).
  \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{definition}
  For $0\leq r\leq n$, consider, $\bigwedge^r\Ouv$. Let $e_i\in\Ouv^n$ denote
  \begin{equation*}
    e_i = (\delta_{ij})_{j=1,\cdots,n}.
  \end{equation*}
  The basis for $\bigwedge^r\Ouv$ is 
  \begin{equation*}
    e_I = e_{i_1}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{i_r},
  \end{equation*}
  where $1\leq i_1<\cdots<i_r\leq n$. We also have,
  \begin{equation*}
    \bigwedge^1\Ouv = \Ouv^n,\bigwedge^r\Ouv = \Ouv^{{n\choose r}}, \bigwedge^n\Ouv = \Ouv.
  \end{equation*}
  We then define,
  \begin{equation*}
    \bigwedge^r\Ouv\stackrel{d_r}{\to}\bigwedge^{r-1}\Ouv, d_re_{i_1}\wedge\cdots e_{i_r} = \sum_{j=1}^r (-1)^j z_{i_j}e_{i_1}\wedge\cdots e_{i_{j-1}}\wedge e_{i_{j+1}}\wedge\cdots e_{i_r}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
  By some calculations, we can show that $d_{r-1}d_r=0$.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
  The following sequence is called a Kossul complex.
  \label{definition_kossul_complex}
  \begin{equation*}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \bigwedge^n\Ouv^n \arrow[r, "d_n"] & \bigwedge^{n-1}\Ouv^n \arrow[r, "d_{n-1}"] & \cdots \arrow[r, "d_2"] & \bigwedge^1\Ouv^n \arrow[r, "d_1"] & \mathcal{M} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}\tag{K}\label{equation_kossul_complex}
  \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
  The sequence \eqref{equation_kossul_complex} is exact. 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  See \cite{Gr-Ra}.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}[Gleason]
  Let $\G$ be pseudoconvex in $\C^n$ containing the origin. Let $M$ be such that 
  \begin{equation*}
    M \defeq \{f\in\Ouv(\G)\sep f(0)=0\}.
  \end{equation*}
  Then we have, 
  \begin{equation*}
    M = (z_1,\cdots,z_n)\Ouv(\G) = M.
  \end{equation*}
  That is for any $f\in M$, we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    f=\sum_{i=1}^n f_iz_i,
  \end{equation*}
  for some $f_1,\cdots,f_n\in\Ouv(\G)$.
\end{theorem}
Note that $\mathcal{M}_x = \{f_x\sep f_x\}$.
\begin{proof}
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}_n \arrow[r] & \Ouv^n \arrow[r, "{\substack{(f_1,\cdots,f_n)\mapsto \sum_{i=1}^n f_i\\\:}}"] & \mathcal{M} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
  Observe that $\mathcal{M}(\G)=M$. Now the question is 
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
\cdots \arrow[r] & \Ouv^n(\G) \arrow[r] & \mathcal{M}(\G)=M \arrow[r] & {\underbrace{H^1(\G,\mathcal{K}_n)}_{=0?}} \arrow[r] & \cdots
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center} 
  Using the Kossul complex of $\Ouv^n$, we obtain,
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}_1 \arrow[r]                   & \bigwedge^1\Ouv^n \arrow[r]     & \mathcal{M} \arrow[r]       & 0      \\
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}_2 \arrow[r]                   & \bigwedge^2\Ouv^n \arrow[r]     & \mathcal{K}_1 \arrow[r]     & 0      \\
\vdots      & \vdots                                    & \vdots                          & \vdots                      & \vdots \\
0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}_{n-1} \arrow[r]               & \bigwedge^{n-1}\Ouv^n \arrow[r] & \mathcal{K}_{n-2} \arrow[r] & 0      \\
0 \arrow[r] & \underbrace{\mathcal{K}_n}_{=0} \arrow[r] & \bigwedge^n\Ouv^n \arrow[r]     & \mathcal{K}_{n-1} \arrow[r] & 0     
\end{tikzcd}
  \end{center}
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  Let $\G$ be a domain containing $0$ and 
  \begin{equation*}
    A(\G)\defeq\{f\in\mathcal{C}^0(\overline{\G})\sep \text{$f$ is holomorphic on $\G$}\}.
  \end{equation*} 
  Consider $M \defeq\{f\in A(\G)\sep f(0)=0\}$. If $\G$ is strictly pseudoconvex, then $M = (z_1,\cdots,z_n)A(\G)$ (i.e. $M$ is an ideal generated by $z_1,\cdots,z_n$).
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
  $\bigwedge^r \Ouv^n$ is acyclic and we have,
  \begin{equation*}
    H^1(\G,\mathcal{K}_1)\cong H^2(\G,\mathcal{K}_2)\cong H^3(\G,\mathcal{K}_3)\cong \cdots\cong \underbrace{H^n(\G,\mathcal{K}_n)}_{=0}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{remark}
We could also take an equivalent approach. This is done by directly using double complex and $\CR$-equations. This approach can be found in the last chapter of \cite{LiM}.
\begin{definition}
  We define following subsets of holomorphic $p$-forms on a domain $\G$.
  \begin{enumerate}[1).]
    \item $Z^p_{\hol}(\G)\defeq\{f\in\cinf_{p,0}(\G)\sep \text{$f$ is holomorphic and }\partial f=0\}$ (i.e. the set of holomorphic $\partial$-closed $p$-forms). 
    \item $B_{\hol}^p(\G)\defeq\{f\in\cinf_{p,0}(\G)\sep \text{$f$ is holomorphic and }\exists u\in \cinf_{p-1,0},f=\partial u\}$ (i.e. the coboundaries).
  \end{enumerate}
  We then define,
  \begin{equation*}
    H_{\dR,\hol}^p(\G) \defeq Z_{\hol}^p(\G)/B_{\hol}^p(\G),
  \end{equation*}
  which is called the holomorphic de Rham group.
  \end{definition}
  \begin{corollary}
    For a domain of holomorphy $\G$ in $\C^n$, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
      p>n\Rightarrow H^p(\G,\C)=0.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{lemma}
    Let $f\in\cinf_{r}(\G)$ and suppose that $df\in\cinf_{r+1,0}(\G)$. Then there is $\tilde{f}\in\cinf_{r,0}(\G)$ such that,
    \begin{equation*}
      \exists g\in\cinf_{r-1,0}(\G)\st f-\tilde{f}=dg.
    \end{equation*}
    \label{lemma_cohomology_decomposition_pseudoconvex}
  \end{lemma}
  \begin{proof}
    Write,
    \begin{equation*}
      f = \sum_{q=0}^k f_{r-q,q},
    \end{equation*}
    where $0\leq k\leq r$. Induction with respect to $k$ we have for $k=0$, $f= f_{r,0}$. Thus take $\tilde{f}=f$, we have the statement. On the induction step,
    \begin{equation*}
      \overline{\partial}f_{r-k,k}=0,
    \end{equation*}
    thus there is $u\in\cinf_{r-k,k-1}(\G)$ such that $\cbd u = f$. Then,
    \begin{align*}
      f-du & = f-\bd u-\cbd u,\\
      & = \sum_{q=0}^{k-1}f_{r-q,q}-\bd u,\\
      & = \tilde{f}+dv,\\
      &\Rightarrow f=\tilde{f}+d(u+v).
    \end{align*}
    Here $\tilde{f}\in\cinf_{r,0}(\G)$ is the one we get by the induction hypothesis.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{theorem}
    If $\G$ is a pseudoconvex domain in $\C^n$, then 
    \begin{equation*}
      H^p_{\dR,\hol}(\G)\cong \underbrace{H_{\dR}^p(\G)\cong H^p(\G,\C)}_{\text{de Rham}}.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Let $f$ be a $d$-closed $r$-form. Then by Lemma \ref{lemma_cohomology_decomposition_pseudoconvex}, we obtain $\tilde{f}\in\cinf_{r,0}(\G)$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
    f-\tilde{f}=dg.
    \end{equation*}
    By assumption $d\tilde{f}=0$ so $\bd \tilde{f},\cbd\tilde{f}=0$. Therefore $f$ is a holomorphic $r$-form. 
    \par We now show that $f$ is exact from $\tilde{f}$ being $\bd$-exact. Clearly if $f=dg$, then $\tilde{f}=d\tilde{g}$. Using Lemma \ref{lemma_cohomology_decomposition_pseudoconvex} to $\tilde{g}$, there is $\hat{g}_{r-1,0}$ with 
    \begin{equation*}
    \tilde{g}-\hat{g}=dv.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
      d\hat{g}-d\tilde{g}=\tilde{f},\cbd\hat{g}=0,d\hat{g}=\bd\hat{g}=\tilde{f}.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus this yields a map,
    \begin{equation*}
      H_{\dR}^r\stackrel{\substack{f\mapsto \tilde{f}\\\:}}{\to} H_{\hol,\dR}^r.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{proof}
  \subsection{Bounded, in Particular, strictly pseudoconvex, and pseudo convex domains}
  \begin{definition}
    Let $\G$ be a bounded domain in $\R^n$. Suppose $U$ is a neighborhood of $\bd\G$. A function $r:U\to\R$ is said to define $\G$ or a defining function of $\G$ if  
    \begin{align*}
      \G&=\{x\in U\sep r(x)<0\}\cup(\G\backslash U),\\
      \bd\G &= \{x\in U\sep r(x)=0\}.
    \end{align*}
  \end{definition}
    \begin{lemma}
      Let $\G\subseteq\R^n$ be a domain given by the defining function $r:U\to\R$. Set 
      \begin{equation*}
        r_j = {\frac 1 {\pd{r}{x_j}}}, \omega_j = (-1)^j dx_1\wedge\cdots dx_{j-1}\wedge dx_{j+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_n.
      \end{equation*}
      We have,
    \begin{equation*}
      {\frac 1 {r_j}}\omega_j\circ \iota = {\frac 1 {r_k}}\omega_k\circ \iota.
    \end{equation*}
    \label{lemma_surface_element_uniqueness}
  \end{lemma}
  \begin{proof}
    Use $dr\circ\iota = 0$. 
  \end{proof}
  \begin{theorem}[General Stokes' Formula]
    Let $\G$ be a bounded domain whose boundary is piecewise smooth 
    More precisely, there is a neighborhood $U$ of $\bd \G$ and a functi on$r:U\to \R$ such that $dr\not=0$ and we have,
    \begin{align*}
      \G&=\{x\in U\sep r(x)<0\}\cup(\G\backslash U),\\
      \bd\G &= \{x\in U\sep r(x)=0\}.
    \end{align*}
    Such $r$ is called the defining function.
  \end{theorem}
  \setcounter{claim}{0}
  \begin{proof}
    We may assme that 
    \begin{equation*}
      \vert dr\vert=\left(\sum_{j=1}^N\left(\pd{r}{x_j}\right)^2\right)^{{\frac 1 2}},
    \end{equation*}
    and $\vert dr\vert|_{\bd\G}\equiv 1$. Set 
    \begin{equation*}
      \omega_j = (-1)^j dx_1\wedge\cdots dx_{j-1}\wedge dx_{j+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_n.
    \end{equation*}
    Then 
  \begin{equation*}
    dx_j\wedge\omega_j = dV,
  \end{equation*}
  which is the volume form. Let $\iota:\bd\G\to\R^n$ be an embedding. By denoting $\pd{r}{x_j}=r_j$, we have,
  \end{proof} 
  More general version of Stokes' theorem can be found in \cite{Li-Gr}.
  \begin{definition}
    Let $\G\subseteq\R^n$ be a bounded domain given by the defining function $r:U\to \R$. The surface element of $\bd\G$ is defined as,
    \begin{equation*}
      dS\defeq {\frac 1 {\pd{r}{x_j}}}\omega.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}
  \begin{lemma}
    Let $f$ be a $n-1$-form (i.e. $f=\sum_{i=1}^nf_j\omega_j$). Then we have,
    \begin{equation*}
      \int_{\bd\G}f = \int_{\bd\G}\sum_{j=1}^n (f_jr_j)dS.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{lemma}
  We pass these results to $\C^n=\R^{2n}$. 
  \begin{definition}
    Let $\G$ be a domain given by the defining function $r:U\to \R$. The surface element $dS$ is a $2n-1$-form on $\bd\G$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
      \pd{r}{z_j}dS = {\frac 1 2}(\omega_{2j-1}-i\omega_{2j}),\pd{r}{\overline{z}_j}dS = {\frac 1 2}(\omega_{2j-1}+i\omega_{2j}).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}
  \begin{definition}
    Suppose we have,
    \begin{equation*}
    f = \sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\ \vert\alpha\vert=q+1}}f_\alpha d\overline{z}^\alpha,
  \end{equation*}
  then, we define,
  \begin{equation*}
    \vartheta f \defeq -\sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\ \vert\alpha\vert=q}}\sum_{j=1}^n\pd{f_{j\alpha}}{z_j}d\overline{z}^\alpha.
  \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}
  
  Recall that the Hermitial product on $\G\subseteq\C^n$ is defined by,
  \begin{equation*}
    (f,g) = \int_{\G}\langle f,g\rangle d\lambda,
  \end{equation*}
  where 
  \begin{equation*}
    \langle f,g\rangle = \sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\ \vert\alpha\vert=q+1}}f_\alpha\overline{g}_\alpha.
  \end{equation*}

  \begin{lemma}[Partial Integration]
    Let $r:U\to\R$ be a function that defines a bounded domain $\G$ and $u,v$ be functions defined on $\overline{\G}$ and $f,g$ differential forms. Then we have,
    \begin{align*}
      \left(\pd{u}{\overline{z}_j},v\right)+\left(u,\pd{v}{\overline{z}_j}\right)& = \int_{\bd \G}u\overline{v}\pd{v}{\overline{z}_j}dS.\\
      \left(\pd{u}{z_j},v\right)+\left(u,\pd{v}{z_j}\right)& = \int_{\bd \G}u\overline{v}\pd{v}{z_j}dS.\\
      (\vartheta f,g) - (f,\overline{\bd}g) = \int_{\bd\G}\langle\vartheta(rf),g\rangle dS.
    \end{align*}
    \label{lemma_parital_integration}
  \end{lemma}

  \begin{corollary}
    In the setting of Lemma \ref{lemma_parital_integration}, if $f$ or $g$ has a compact support then,
    \begin{equation*}
      (\vartheta f,g) = (f,\cbd g).
    \end{equation*}
    That is $\vartheta$ is a formal adjoint of $\cbd$. 
  \end{corollary}

  \begin{remark}
    We have studied the following sequence,
    \begin{center}
      \begin{tikzcd}
{L_{0,q}^2(\G)} \arrow[r, "T"] & {L_{0,q+1}^2(\G)} \arrow[r, "S"] & {L_{0,q+2}^2(\G)}
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    We will denote the Hilbert adjoint of $\cbd$ as $\cbd^*$. The adjoint is defined as follows. For $f\in\Dom\cbd^*,g\in\Dom\bd$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
      (\cbd^*f,g)=(f,\cbd g).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{remark}

  In order to have $\cbd^*=\vartheta$ on $\mathcal{C}^2(\overline{\G})$, we need,
  \begin{equation*}
    \vartheta(rf)\equiv 0\quad(\text{on $\bd\G$}).
  \end{equation*}

  \begin{definition}
    For $l\in\Z_{\geq0}$, we define,
    \begin{equation*}
      \mathcal{D}_{\cbd^*}^l\defeq\Dom\cbd^*\cap\mathcal{C}^l(\overline{\G}).
    \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}

  \begin{definition}
    The following equation is called the first Neumann condition.
    \par Let $r$ be a function defining a bounded domain $\G$, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
      \forall\alpha\in\N^{n}\st\vert\alpha\vert=q,\text{ we have } \sum_{j=1}^n f_{j\alpha}\pd{r}{z_j}=0\text{ on $\G$}.\tag{N1}\label{equation_first_neumann_condition}
    \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}

  \begin{lemma}
    For $l\geq1$ and $f\in L_{0,q+1}^2(\G)$, we have, $f\in\mathcal{D}_{\cbd^*}^l$ if and only if $\vartheta(rf)=0$ on $\bd\G$. That is $f$ satisfies \eqref{equation_first_neumann_condition}.
  \end{lemma}

  \begin{lemma}
    Let $\varphi\geq0$ be a weight function and $\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^2(\G)$. We note that $\varphi\equiv0$ is also allowed. We define $L_{\cdot,\cdot}^2(\G,\varphi),\vartheta_\varphi$, accordingly. Then the first Neumann condition does not depends on $\varphi$.
  \end{lemma}

  \begin{definition}
    Let $f\in\Dom\cbd^*_\varphi\cap\cinf(\G)$. The graph norm of $f$ is defined as,
    \begin{equation*}
      \norm{f}+\norm{\cbd f}+\norm{\cbd^* f}.
    \end{equation*}
  \end{definition}

  \begin{theorem}[Hörmander-Friedrich]
    Let $\G,\bd\G$ defined by $r:U\to\R$. We have, $\Dom\cbd^*_\varphi\cap\cinf(\G)$ is dense in $\Dom\cbd^*\cap\Dom\cbd$. That is for any $f\in \Dom\cbd^*\cap\Dom\cbd$, there is a sequence $(f_j)\in\cinf(\G)$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
      f_j\stackrel{L^2}{\to}f,\cbd f_j\stackrel{L^2}{\to}\cbd f,\vartheta f_j\stackrel{L^2}{\to}\cbd^*f,
    \end{equation*}
    and each $f_j$ satisfies \eqref{equation_first_neumann_condition}. 
  \end{theorem}

  \begin{proof}
    The proof can be found in \cite{LiM} chapter 5, section 4.
  \end{proof}
  What we want to do now is to replicate the calculation we have done before to calculate adjoints but with only one weight function $\varphi$, but taking accout of boundary integrals. In general boundary integral $=0$ because of (missing) (some integrals).
  \begin{theorem}[Kohn-Morrey]
    Let $\G,\bd\G$ be defined by $r$ and $\varphi\in\cinf(\G)$ be such that $\varphi\geq0$. Then for 
    \begin{equation*}
    f=\sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\\vert\alpha\vert=q+1}}f_\alpha d\overline{z}^\alpha,
    \end{equation*}
    which is smooth on $\overline{\G}$ and satisfies \eqref{equation_first_neumann_condition}, we have,
    \begin{align*}
      \norm{\cbd f}^2_\varphi+\norm{\cbd_\varphi^*f}^2_\varphi&=\int_\G\sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\\vert\alpha\vert=q}}\sum_{j,k}{\frac {\partial^2\varphi}{\partial z_j\partial\overline{z}_k}}f_{j\alpha}\overline{f}_{k\alpha}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda\\
      &+\int_\G\sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\\vert\alpha\vert=q+1}}\sum_j\left\vert\pd{f_j}{\overline{z}_j}\right\vert^2e^{-\varphi}d\lambda\\
      &+\int_{\bd\G}\sum_{\substack{\alpha\in\N^n\\\vert\alpha\vert=q}}\sum_{j,k}{\frac {\partial^2 r}{\partial z_j\bd \overline{z}_k}}f_{j\alpha}\overline{f}_{k\alpha}e^{-\varphi}dS.
    \end{align*}
    \label{theorem_kohn_morrey}
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{corollary}
    Take $\varphi\equiv0$. Suppose $\G$ is a strictly pseudoconvex domain defined by a strictly plurisubharmonic function $r$. We then have,
    \begin{equation*}
      \norm{\cbd f}^2+\norm{\cbd^* f}^2\geq c\int_{\bd\G}\vert f\vert^2 dS.
    \end{equation*}
    This equation is called Kohn's basic estimate.
  \end{corollary}
  From now on, we will only use the first term of Theorem \ref{theorem_kohn_morrey}. That is 
  \begin{equation*}
      \norm{\cbd f}_\varphi^2+\norm{\cbd_\varphi^* f}^2\geq c\norm{f}_\varphi^2.
    \end{equation*}
    \begin{theorem}
      Let $\G\compembed\C^n$ be a pseudoconvex domain. Then there is a constant $c>0$, only depending on the diameter of $\G$, such that for any $f\in L^2_{0,q+1}(\G)$, $\cbd f=0$, there is $u\in L_{0,q}^2(\G)$, with $\cbd u=f$ and $\norm{u}\leq c\norm{f}$.
      \par If $f$ is smooth then $u$ can be chosen as a smooth function.
    \end{theorem}

    In the equality of Theorem \ref{theorem_kohn_morrey}, instead of considering the whole, we may take only a single summation term on the right hand side, we aim to derive the following inequality.

    \begin{theorem}[Adjusted Kohn-Morrey]
      Let $\G$ be a strictly pseudoconvex domain and $\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^2(\overline{\G})$ be a weight function. Then for $f\in L^2_{0,q}(\G,\varphi)$, we have,
      \begin{equation*}
        \int\sum_K\sum_{j,k}{\frac {\partial^2\varphi}{\partial z_j\overline{\partial}\overline{z}_k}}f_{jK}\overline{f}_{kK}e^{-\varphi}d\lambda\leq\norm{\cbd f}_\varphi^2+\norm{\cbd_\varphi^*f}^2_{\varphi}.
      \end{equation*}
    \end{theorem}

    \begin{proof}
      choose $\varphi(z) = t\norm{z}^2$ and $t>0$ will be chosen later.
      \begin{equation*}
        t\norm{f}^2_\varphi\leq\norm{\cbd f}_\varphi^2+\norm{\cbd^*_\varphi}_{\varphi}^2. 
      \end{equation*}
      In a favorable situation, we have,
      \begin{equation*}
        L_{0,q+1}^2(\G,\varphi) = \mathcal{N}_{\cbd_\varphi^*}\oplus\mathcal{R}_{\cbd},L^2_{0,q}(\G,\varphi) = \mathcal{N}_{\cbd}\oplus\mathcal{R}_{\cbd^*_\varphi}. 
      \end{equation*}
      Therefore for $f\in L^2(\G,\varphi)_{0,q+1}$ with $\cbd f=0$, we have $f\in\mathcal{R}_{\cbd}$. That is $f=\cbd u$ for some $u\in\Dom\cbd\subseteq L_{0,q}^2(\G,\varphi)$.
      We may assume that 
      \begin{equation*}
        u=\cbd_\varphi^*v,v\in\Dom\cbd^*_\varphi,
      \end{equation*}
      and $\cbd v=0$. Apply this to $v$, we have,
      \begin{align*}
        t\norm{v}^2_\varphi&\leq\norm{\cbd_\varphi^* v}_\varphi^2,\\
        \norm{u}_{\varphi}^2&=(u,u)_\varphi = (u,\cbd_\varphi^* v)_\varphi,\\
        & = (\cbd u,v)_\varphi=(f,v)_\varphi,\\
        & \leq \norm{f}_\varphi\norm{v}_{\varphi},\\
        &\leq {\frac 1{\sqrt{t}}}\norm{f}_\varphi\norm{\cbd_\varphi^* v}_\varphi,\\
        & = {\frac 1{\sqrt{t}}}\norm{f}_\varphi\norm{u}_\varphi.
      \end{align*}
      Therefore, we have a solution $u$ with,
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{u}_\varphi\leq{\frac 1 {\sqrt{t}}}\norm{f}_\varphi.
      \end{equation*}
      Compute,
      \begin{align*}
        \int_\G\vert u\vert^2e^{-t\norm{z}^2}d\lambda & = {\frac 1 t}\int_\G \vert f\vert^2e^{-t\norm{z}^2}d\lambda,\\
        & \leq {\frac 1 t}\int_\G \vert f\vert^2 d\lambda.
      \end{align*}
      For $\delta>\diam\G$, we have,
      \begin{equation*}
        e^{-t\delta^2}\int_\G\vert u\vert^2d\lambda \leq \int_\G\vert u\vert^2e^{-t\norm{z}^2}d\lambda.
      \end{equation*}
      This implies,
      \begin{equation*}
        \int_\G\vert u\vert^2d\lambda \leq {\frac {e^{t\delta^2}} t}\int_\G \vert f\vert^2d\lambda, \norm{u}^2\leq e\delta^2\norm{f}^2.
      \end{equation*}
      As $\G$ is bounded and pseudoconvex, take $\delta=\diam\G$, we may take a exhaustion by strictly pseudoconvex domains,
      \begin{equation*}
        \G_1\subset\G_2\subset\cdots\subset\G.
      \end{equation*}
      For $f\in L^2_{0,q+1}(\G)$, solve the Cauchy-Riemann equation on each domain $\G_i$ and denote the solution $u_i$, (i.e. $\cbd u_i=f$ on $\G_i$) with the inequality,
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{u_i}^2\leq e\delta^2\norm{f}^2.
      \end{equation*}
      Choose $t={\frac 1 {\delta^2}}$. Now we have a sequence of bounded $L^2$ functions $(u_i)_{i\in \N}$. Without loss of genearlity, we assume
      \begin{equation*}
        u_i\stackrel{\text{\makebox[0pt][c]{weakly in $L^2$}}}{\longrightarrow} u\in L_{0,q}^2(\G), u_i\stackrel{\mathcal{D}'}{\to}u.
      \end{equation*}
      Of course we have,
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{u}^2\leq e\delta^2\norm{f}^2.
      \end{equation*}
      Furthermore, for $i\geq j$, we have,
      \begin{equation*}
        \cbd u_i=\cbd u_j\text{ on $\G_j$} \Rightarrow \cbd u = f \text{ on $\G$}.
      \end{equation*}
    \end{proof}
    \begin{theorem}
      Let $\G\compembed\C^n$ be pseudoconvex and $f\in L^2_{0,q+1}(\G),\cbd f=0$. Then there is $u\in L^2_{0,q}(\G)$ such that 
      \begin{equation*}
        \cbd u = f,\norm{u}^2\leq e\delta^2\norm{f}^2.
      \end{equation*}
      $u$ carries any regularity that $f$ has.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{notation}
      $\norm{\cdot}_{W^k}$ denotes the Sobolev norm of order $k$.
    \end{notation}
    \begin{lemma}
      Let $u\in W^s(\R^n)$ for $s\geq0$ and let us denote its Fourier transform $\hat{u}$. We have,
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{u}_{W^s}=\norm{\hat{u}(1+\norm{x}^2)^{{\frac s 2}}}.
      \end{equation*}
    \end{lemma}
    \begin{definition}
      Let $\G$ be a strictly pseudoconex domain. The Bergman projection is an orthogonal projection,
      \begin{equation*}
        P:L^2(\G)\to L^2(\G)\cap\Ouv(\G).
      \end{equation*}
    \end{definition}
    \begin{theorem}[Kohn]
      Let $\G\compembed\C^n$ be a strictly pseudoconvex domain and $f\in L_{0,q}^2(\G)$ for $q\geq1$. 
      \begin{enumerate}[1).]
        \item If $f\in\Dom\cbd\cap\Dom\cbd^*$, we have,
        \begin{equation*}
          \norm{f}_{W^{k+{\frac 1 2}}} \leq c(\norm{\cbd f}_{W^k}+\norm{\cbd^* f}_{W^k}+\norm{f}).
        \end{equation*}
        \item There is $c>0$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
          \norm{u-Pu}_{W^{k+{\frac 1 2}}}\leq c(\norm{\cbd u}_{W^k}+\norm{u}_{W^k})
        \end{equation*}
      \end{enumerate}
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}[Sobolev]
      Let $\G\compembed\R^N$ be a domain and $d=\left[{\frac N 2}\right]+1$. Then 
      \begin{equation*}
        W^{s+d}(\overline{\G})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^s(\overline{\G}),
      \end{equation*} 
      where $\mathcal{C}^s(\overline{\G})$ denotes the space of functions whose derivative of order up to $s$ are bounded.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}
      Let $\G$ be strictly pseudoconevx with $\bd\G$ smooth and $f\in \cinf_{0,q}(\G)$ with $\cbd f=0,q\geq1$. Then there is $u\in\cinf_{0,q+1}(\overline{\G})$ with $\cbd u = f$.
      Moreover, the canonical solution $u$ satisfying either 
      \begin{equation*}
        \begin{cases}
          \vartheta u = 0,\\
          Pu = 0,
        \end{cases}
      \end{equation*}
      has this property. (Recall $\vartheta$ is formal adjoint of $\cbd$).
    \end{theorem}
    For $f$ defined on $\G\compembed\C$, set 
    \begin{equation*}
      u(z) = \int_\G C(\zeta,z)f(\zeta).
    \end{equation*}
    %find what properties $u$ carries.
    Now take $f$ as a $(p,q)$-form on $\G$. We want to find a kernel $K(\zeta,z)$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
      u(z) = \int_\G f(\zeta)K(\zeta,z), \cbd u(z)=f(z).
    \end{equation*}
    \begin{theorem}
      Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a strictly pseudoconvex domain and $f_0\in L^\infty_{0,1}(\G)\cap\cinf(\G)$ with $\cbd f = 0$. Then there is a solution $u\in L^\infty(\G)\cap\cinf(\G)$ with $\cbd u = f$. Furthermore, there is a constant $C\geq0$, which is independent of $f$ such that 
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{u}_{L^\infty}\leq C\norm{f}_{L^\infty}.
      \end{equation*}
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{remark}[Henkin-Ramírez]
      $C$ is invariant under small pertubation of $\G$ but not under larger pertubations.
    \end{remark}
    \begin{definition}
      Let $f:\R\to\R$. The Hölder norm of $f$ order $k$ is 
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}^k}\defeq\inf\{C\geq0\sep \forall x,y\in\R,\vert f(x)-f(y)\vert\leq C\vert x-y\vert^{{\frac 1 2}}\}.
      \end{equation*}
    \end{definition}
    \begin{theorem}
      Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a strictly pseudoconvex domain. There is a linear continuous operator $S: L_{0,q+1}^1\to L_{0,q}^1$ with 
      \begin{equation*}
        \cbd f=0\Rightarrow \cbd Sf = f. 
      \end{equation*}
      $Sf$ is smooth in $\G$ if $f$ is smooth (i.e. $S$ is the solution operator).
      \begin{equation*}
        \norm{Sf}_{\mathcal{C}^{k+{\frac 1 2}}}\leq \const\norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}^k}.
      \end{equation*}
      This constant is invariant under small pertubations.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{proof}
      The proof can be found in \cite{LiM}.
    \end{proof}
    \begin{theorem}
      \label{theorem_hermitian_metric_holder_norm_inequality}
      Let $\G\subseteq\C^n$ be a strictly pseudoconex domain, then there is a Hermitian metric on $\C^n$ such that 
      \begin{enumerate}[i).]
        \item for $f\in L^2_{0,q+1}(\G),f\in\Dom\cbd\cap\Dom\cbd^*$, 
        \begin{equation*}
          \norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}^{k+{\frac 1 2}}}\leq \const(\norm{\cbd f}_{\mathcal{C}^k}+\norm{\cbd^*f}_{\mathcal{C}^k}+\norm{f}_{L^2}).
        \end{equation*}
        \item for $f\in L_{0,0}^2(\G)$, we have,
        \begin{equation*}
          \norm{f-Pf}_{\cC^{k+{\frac 1 2}}}\leq\const (\norm{\cbd f}_{\cC^k}+\norm{f}_{L^2}).
        \end{equation*}
      \end{enumerate}
      Note that the metric is used to define $L^2$-space, the projection $P$, and the adjoint $\cbd^*$.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{remark}[Beals-Greiner-Stanton]$\:$
      \par The Theorem \ref{theorem_hermitian_metric_holder_norm_inequality} holds for arbitrary metrics.
    \end{remark}
    \section{What now?}
    \subsection{Stein Manifolds}
    We will examine how the results we have seen can be studied further. One canonical generalization of these results would be to consider complex manifolds instead of $\C^n$. 
    \begin{definition}
      Let $X$ be a $n$-dimensional complex manifold. $X$ is said to be Stein if 
      \begin{enumerate}[i).]
        \item it has countable topology (i.e. admits a countable basis of the topology),
        \item it is holomorphically convex,
        \item it is holomorphically separable (i.e. for any $x\not=y,\exists f\in\Ouv(X)$ such that $f(x)\not=f(y)$),
        \item for all $x\in X$, there is a global holomorphic coordinates (i.e. there is $f_1,\cdots,f_m\in\Ouv(X)$ that give local coordinate at $x$).
      \end{enumerate}
    \end{definition}
    \begin{remark}
      $\G\subseteq\C^n$ is a Stein manifold if and only if it is a domain of holomorphy.
    \end{remark}
    Our motivation is to show natural domains of existence for holomorphic functions (in $\C^n$) are complex manifold $X\stackrel{P}{\to}\C^n$ where $X$ is an unbranced Riemann domain.
    \begin{definition}
      $X$ is pseudoconvex if there is smooth, strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustive functions.
    \end{definition}
    \begin{theorem}
      $X$ is Stein if and only if it is pseudoconvex.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{proof}
      We will not give the full proof, however we will note some tools which we need to utilise to show the statement. Namely, $\cbd$-complex, $(p,q)$-forms, instead of $(0,q)$-forms, $L^2$ spaces with respect to a hermitian metrix.
    \end{proof}
    \begin{theorem}[Cartan-Thullen-Oka]
      $X$ is a domain of holomorphy of $\C^n$ then $X$ is Stein.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}[Behnke-Stein]
      Open Riemann surfaces are Stein.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}[Remmert]
       $X$ is $n$-dimensional Stein manifold if and only if $\phi:X\to\C^{2n+1}$ biholomorphic embedding.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}
      The second and second Cousin problems are solvable in $\C^n$.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}[Cartan-Serre]
      If $S$ is a coherent analytic sheaf on a Stein manifold $X$, then $H^q(X,S)=0$ for all $q\geq1$.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{remark}
      In the first and the last condition of Stein manifolds are redundant. 
    \end{remark}
    \begin{theorem}[Grauert]
      \label{theorem_grauert_stein}
      $X$ is Stein if and only if it is holomorphically convex and for all $x\in X$, there are holormophic functions $f_1,\cdots,f_k\in\Ouv(X)$ such that $x$ is isolated in $V(f_1,\cdots,f_k)$.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}[Radó]
      All connected Riemann surfaces have countable topology.
    \end{theorem}
    \subsection{Complex Spaces}
    \begin{definition}
      A topological space $X$ is Stein if it is holomorphically convex and for all $x\in X$, there are holormophic functions $f_1,\cdots,f_k\in\Ouv(X)$ such that $x$ is isolated in $V(f_1,\cdots,f_k)$ (as per Theorem \ref{theorem_grauert_stein}).
    \end{definition}
    \begin{theorem}
      A topological space $X$ is Stein if and only if it is pseudoconvex.
    \end{theorem}
    \begin{theorem}[Cartan-Serre]
      If $S$ is a coherent analytic sheaf on a Stein space $X$, then $H^q(X,S)=0$ for all $q\geq1$.
    \end{theorem}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $S\subset X$ be a subset. Suppose $X\backslash S$ is a connected $n$-dimensional manifold. One can use the arsenal of differential forms to study this and consider $S$ on a boundary which can be totally pathological.
    \begin{theorem}[Milestones of Complex Analysis]$\:$
      \begin{enumerate}[I).]
        \item (Hironaka) Let $X$ be a complex space, then there is $X'\stackrel{\pi}{\to}X$ where $X'$ is a complex manifold and $\pi$ is holomorphic and biholomorphic over the regular points. That is to say, we can desingularize complex spaces.
        \item (Grauert) Let $p:X\to Y$ be a holomorphic map between complex spaces which is proper (i.e. each compact set in $Y$ has its preimage compact in $X$ under $p$). Then $R^ip\mathcal{S}$ where $\mathcal{S}$ is coherent sheaf on $X$ are coherent.
      \end{enumerate}
    \end{theorem}
